#10744. Think AI "knows" what it’s doing? Scientists say think again

These are everyday words people use to describe what goes on in the human mind. But when those same terms are applied to artificial intelligence, they can unintentionally make machines seem more human than they really are.

"We use mental verbs all the time in our daily lives, so it makes sense that we might also use them when we talk about machines -- it helps us relate to them," said Jo Mackiewicz, professor of English at Iowa State. "But at the same time, when we apply mental verbs to machines, there's also a risk of blurring the line between what humans and AI can do."

Mackiewicz and Jeanine Aune, a teaching professor of English and director of the advanced communication program at Iowa State, are part of a research team that studied how writers describe AI using human-like language. This type of wording, known as anthropomorphism, assigns human traits to non-human systems. Their study, "Anthropomorphizing Artificial Intelligence: A Corpus Study of Mental Verbs Used with AI and ChatGPT," was published in Technical Communication Quarterly.

The research team also included Matthew J. Baker, associate professor of linguistics at Brigham Young University, and Jordan Smith, assistant professor of English at the University of Northern Colorado. Both previously studied at Iowa State University.

Why Human-Like Language About AI Can Be Misleading

According to the researchers, using mental verbs to describe AI can create a false impression. Words such as "think," "know," "understand," and "want" suggest that a system has thoughts, intentions, or awareness. In reality, AI does not possess beliefs or feelings. It produces responses by analyzing patterns in data, not by forming ideas or making conscious decisions.

Mackiewicz and Aune also pointed out that this kind of language can overstate what AI is capable of. Phrases like "AI decided" or "ChatGPT knows" can make systems seem more independent or intelligent than they actually are. This can lead to unrealistic expectations about how reliable or capable AI is.

There is also a broader concern. When AI is described as if it has intentions, it can distract from the humans behind it. Developers, engineers, and organizations are responsible for how these systems are built and used.

"Certain anthropomorphic phrases may even stick in readers' minds and can potentially shape public perception of AI in unhelpful ways," Aune said.

How News Writers Actually Use AI Language

To better understand how often this kind of language appears, the researchers analyzed the News on the Web (NOW) corpus. This massive dataset contains more than 20 billion words from English-language news articles published in 20 countries.

They focused on how frequently mental verbs such as "learns," "means," and "knows" were used alongside terms like AI and ChatGPT.

Mental Verbs Are Less Common Than Expected

The study found that news writers do not frequently pair AI-related terms with mental verbs.

While anthropomorphism is common in everyday speech, it appears far less often in news writing. "Anthropomorphism has been shown to be common in everyday speech, but we found there's far less usage in news writing," Mackiewicz said.

Among the examples identified, the word "needs" appeared most often with AI, showing up 661 times. For ChatGPT, "knows" was the most frequent pairing, but it appeared only 32 times.

The researchers noted that editorial standards may play a role. Associated Press guidelines, which discourage attributing human emotions or traits to AI, could be influencing how journalists write about these technologies.

Context Matters More Than the Words Themselves

Even when mental verbs were used, they were not always anthropomorphic.

For instance, the word "needs" often described basic requirements rather than human-like qualities. Phrases such as "AI needs large amounts of data" or "AI needs some human assistance" are similar to how people describe non-human systems like cars or recipes. In these cases, the language does not imply that AI has thoughts or desires.

In other cases, "needs" was used to express what should be done, such as "AI needs to be trained" or "AI needs to be implemented." Aune explained that these examples were often written in passive voice, which shifts responsibility back to human actors rather than the technology itself.

The study also showed that not all uses of mental verbs are equal. Some phrases move closer to suggesting human-like qualities.

For example, statements like "AI needs to understand the real world" can imply expectations tied to human reasoning, ethics, or awareness. These uses go beyond simple descriptions and begin to suggest deeper capabilities.

"These instances showed that anthropomorphizing isn't all-or-nothing and instead exists on a spectrum," Aune said

Overall, the researchers found that anthropomorphism in news coverage is both less frequent and more nuanced than many might assume.

"Overall, our analysis shows that anthropomorphization of AI in news writing is far less common -- and far more nuanced -- than we might think," Mackiewicz said. "Even the instances that did anthropomorphize AI varied widely in strength."

The findings highlight the importance of context. Simply counting words is not enough to understand how language shapes meaning.

"For writers, this nuance matters: the language we choose shapes how readers understand AI systems, their capabilities and the humans responsible for them," Mackiewicz said.

The research team also emphasized that these insights can help professionals think more carefully about how they describe AI in their work.

"Our findings can help technical and professional communication practitioners reflect on how they think about AI technologies as tools in their writing process and how they write about AI," the research team wrote in the published study.

As AI continues to develop, the way people talk about it will remain important. Mackiewicz and Aune said writers will need to stay mindful of how word choices influence perception.

Looking ahead, the team suggested that future studies could explore how different words shape understanding and whether even rare uses of anthropomorphic language have a strong impact on how people view AI.

Materials provided by Iowa State University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10743. The Hidden Problems Behind Website-to-CRM Integrations (and How to Prevent Them)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Connecting a website form to a customer relationship management (CRM) system may seem straightforward. A developer collects information from a form, sends it to an API endpoint, and checks whether a new contact appears in the CRM.

The integration may work perfectly during the first test. However, problems can appear after the website goes live. Some leads may not reach the CRM, duplicate contacts may appear, or important customer information may be missing.

These issues are not always easy to notice. A visitor might see a confirmation message even when the CRM has failed to save their information.

This guide explains common problems in website-to-CRM integrations and practical ways developers can prevent them.

A website-to-CRM integration allows information collected through a website to move into a CRM system. This usually happens through an API or webhook.

For example, when someone completes a contact form, the website collects their name, email address, phone number, and other details. The integration then sends this information to the CRM, where it can be stored and used by the relevant team.

An API integration provides a structured way for two applications to exchange information. Developers who need additional background can also read OpenHerald's overview of API integration.

The process usually involves several steps:

The website validates the submitted information.

The application sends a request to the CRM.

The CRM checks the request and processes the data.

The website or backend handles the response.

Each step needs to work correctly. If one part fails, the information may not reach its destination.

One important rule is to avoid displaying a successful submission message before the application has safely accepted and stored the data.

Data issues are among the most frequent problems in website-to-CRM integrations. They may not appear during development because test submissions usually contain simple, predictable information.

Real visitors enter data in different formats and may submit the same form more than once.

A visitor might complete a contact form and later download a resource using the same email address. If the integration creates a new CRM record every time, the same person may appear several times.

To prevent this, developers should decide how existing contacts will be identified.

Email addresses are commonly used for matching. Before comparing them, the application should remove unnecessary spaces and convert the address to a consistent format.

These values should be treated consistently.

Where supported, an upsert operation can create a new contact or update an existing one instead of creating unnecessary duplicates.

Website forms and CRM systems do not always use the same field structure.

A website may collect a visitor's full name in one field, while the CRM expects separate first-name and last-name values. Other fields may have different names or requirements.

Developers should create a clear mapping between website fields and CRM fields before building the integration.

They should also check which fields are required. If the CRM requires information that the website does not collect, the request may fail.

Different systems may store information in different formats.

For example, one application might use United Kingdom, while another expects UK. Dates, phone numbers, and country values can also create problems.

A reliable integration should validate and standardise these values before sending them to the CRM.

A contact record is more useful when the sales or marketing team knows where the visitor came from.

However, integrations sometimes send only basic contact information and leave out details such as campaign source, landing page, and referral information.

Developers should identify which tracking fields are useful and include them in the integration where appropriate.

Even an integration that works correctly at launch can stop working later.

Authentication credentials may expire, permissions may change, or an API provider may limit the number of requests an application can make.

Three areas deserve particular attention.

Many APIs use access tokens or other credentials to verify requests. Some tokens expire and must be refreshed.

If an application does not handle token renewal correctly, requests may begin failing without an obvious problem on the website.

Developers should implement the authentication process recommended by the CRM provider and monitor failed requests.

An API provider may restrict the number of requests allowed within a particular period.

A marketing campaign can suddenly increase website traffic and generate more form submissions than usual. If the integration exceeds the provider's limit, some requests may be rejected.

The application should recognise rate-limit responses and retry requests according to the provider's instructions.

An integration should not receive more access than it needs.

For example, a form submission service may only need permission to create or update contacts. Giving it full administrative access increases unnecessary security risks.

Use the minimum permissions required for the integration to function.

External services can experience outages, network delays, and temporary errors. If the website sends a form submission directly to the CRM without storing it first, a failed request could mean a lost lead.

A safer approach is to separate receiving a submission from delivering it to the CRM.

Validate the form information on the server.

Store the submission safely in your own system.

Send it to the CRM through a background process.

Retry temporary failures after an appropriate delay.

Flag permanent failures for manual review.

This approach gives the application another opportunity to deliver information when the CRM becomes available again.

A request might reach the CRM successfully, but the response could be lost because of a network problem. The website may then retry the same request, creating a duplicate contact.

Idempotency helps prevent this situation. The application can use a stable identifier for each submission so that repeating the same operation does not create another record.

For example, a combination of the form ID and a normalised email address may help identify repeated submissions. The exact approach depends on the CRM's API capabilities.

This is an illustrative example. Functions such as crmUpsertContact() and scheduleRetry() need to be implemented according to the application and CRM being used.

The important point is that every submission should have a known status: successfully delivered, waiting for another attempt, or requiring manual attention.

A working integration should also protect the quality and security of the information it collects.

Public forms can receive automated submissions from bots. Without appropriate protection, these entries can fill the CRM with unwanted records.

Validation should happen on the server, even if the website already checks information in the browser.

Some forms collect permission to send marketing communications.

If a visitor selects a consent checkbox, that information should also be passed to the CRM. Otherwise, the CRM may contain the contact details without a reliable record of the visitor's choice.

Where relevant, store the consent value, the wording or version displayed, and the time the visitor gave consent.

An integration should only send the information needed for its intended purpose.

Collecting unnecessary personal details increases the amount of information the application must protect and maintain.

Developers should also understand where submitted information is stored, including databases, queues, application logs, and the CRM.

Testing an integration before launch is important, but monitoring it afterwards is just as necessary.

A successful test does not prove that every future submission will work.

The following checks can help identify problems early.

Compare the number of website form submissions with the number of contacts successfully created or updated in the CRM.

A growing difference between the two numbers may indicate that some requests are failing.

Application logs should record the reason a request failed and include a submission identifier where possible.

Avoid storing complete personal details in logs unless there is a specific, justified need.

Developers should configure notifications when the integration's failure rate exceeds an acceptable threshold.

This makes it easier to investigate problems before they affect a large number of submissions.

A scheduled test submission can help confirm that the complete process is still working.

For example, a test lead can be sent periodically and checked to confirm that it reaches the CRM correctly.

Someone should be responsible for monitoring the integration.

This person or team should review errors, check API changes, monitor credentials, and investigate changes to CRM fields.

Without a clear owner, small problems can remain unnoticed for a long time.

Documentation makes integrations easier to maintain and troubleshoot.

It does not need to be complicated. A short reference document covering the main rules can save considerable time when another developer needs to work on the system.

Before deployment, developers should also test unusual situations.

Testing these situations helps reveal weaknesses that ordinary form submissions may not expose.

What is the most common website-to-CRM integration problem?

Duplicate contacts and incorrect field mapping are common problems. They usually happen when the website and CRM handle data differently.

How can developers prevent duplicate contacts?

Normalise email addresses, use a consistent matching rule, and use an upsert operation or idempotency mechanism where the CRM supports it.

Should a website send form data directly to a CRM?

For more reliable handling, the application should first validate and safely store the submission. A background process can then deliver it to the CRM and retry temporary failures.

How can I tell if a CRM integration has stopped working?

Compare website submission counts with CRM records, monitor failed requests, and schedule regular test submissions.

Who should be responsible for maintaining the integration?

Assign a developer or technical team to monitor the connection, review errors, maintain credentials, and respond to API changes.

Developers can make these integrations more reliable by validating information on the server, storing submissions before delivery, using safe retry mechanisms, and monitoring the results after launch.

The goal is simple: every valid submission should reach the CRM, and every failed submission should be visible and recoverable rather than silently lost.

#10739. Artificial neurons successfully communicate with living brain cells

Engineers at Northwestern University have created printed artificial neurons that go beyond imitation and can directly interact with real brain cells. These flexible, low-cost devices produce electrical signals that closely resemble those generated by living neurons, allowing them to activate biological brain tissue.

In experiments using slices of mouse brain, the artificial neurons successfully triggered responses in real neurons. This result shows a new level of compatibility between electronic devices and living neural systems.

Toward Brain Interfaces and Energy-Efficient AI

This advance moves researchers closer to electronics that can directly interface with the nervous system. Potential uses include brain-machine interfaces and neuroprosthetics, such as implants that could help restore hearing, vision, or movement.

The technology also points toward a new generation of computing systems inspired by the brain. By replicating how neurons communicate, future hardware could perform complex tasks using far less energy. The brain remains the most energy-efficient computing system known, and scientists hope to apply its principles to modern technology.

The study will be published on April 15 in the journal Nature Nanotechnology.

"The world we live in today is dominated by artificial intelligence (AI)," said Northwestern's Mark C. Hersam, who led the study. "The way you make AI smarter is by training it on more and more data. This data-intensive training leads to a massive power-consumption problem. Therefore, we have to come up with more efficient hardware to handle big data and AI. Because the brain is five orders of magnitude more energy efficient than a digital computer, it makes sense to look to the brain for inspiration for next-generation computing."

Hersam is an expert in brain-inspired computing and holds multiple roles at Northwestern University, including the Walter P. Murphy Professor of Materials Science and Engineering at the McCormick School of Engineering. He also is a professor of medicine at Northwestern University Feinberg School of Medicine and a professor of chemistry at the Weinberg College of Arts and Sciences. In addition, he serves as chair of the department of materials science and engineering, director of the Materials Research Science and Engineering Center, and a member of the International Institute for Nanotechnology. He co-led the study with Vinod K. Sangwan, a research associate professor at McCormick.

Why the Brain Outperforms Traditional Silicon

Modern computers handle increasing workloads by packing billions of identical transistors onto rigid, two-dimensional silicon chips. Each component behaves the same way, and once manufactured, the system remains fixed.

The brain works very differently. It consists of many types of neurons, each with specialized roles, arranged in soft, three-dimensional networks. These networks are constantly changing, forming and adjusting connections as learning occurs.

"Silicon achieves complexity by having billions of identical devices," Hersam said. "Everything is the same, rigid and fixed once it's fabricated. The brain is the opposite. It's heterogeneous, dynamic and three-dimensional. To move in that direction, we need new materials and new ways to build electronics."

Although artificial neurons have been developed before, most produce overly simple signals. To achieve more complex behavior, engineers typically need large networks of devices, which increases energy use.

Printable Materials Enable Brain-Like Behavior

To better replicate real neural activity, Hersam's team built artificial neurons using soft, printable materials that more closely match the brain's structure. Their approach relies on electronic inks made from nanoscale flakes of molybdenum disulfide (MoS2), which acts as a semiconductor, and graphene, which serves as an electrical conductor. These materials were deposited onto flexible polymer surfaces using aerosol jet printing.

Previously, researchers treated the polymer in these inks as a flaw because it interfered with electrical performance. As a result, they removed it after printing. In this work, the team used that same feature to enhance the device.

"Instead of fully removing the polymer, we partially decompose it," he said. "Then, when we pass current through the device, we drive further decomposition of the polymer. This decomposition occurs in a spatially inhomogeneous manner, leading to formation of a conductive filament, such that all the current is constricted into a narrow region in space."

That narrow conductive path produces a sudden electrical response similar to a neuron firing. The resulting device can generate a wide variety of signals, including single spikes, continuous firing, and bursting patterns, closely resembling real neural communication.

Because each artificial neuron can produce more complex signals, fewer components are needed to perform advanced tasks. This could significantly improve computing efficiency.

Testing Artificial Neurons on Real Brain Tissue

To evaluate whether the artificial neurons could truly interact with living systems, the researchers partnered with Indira M. Raman, the Bill and Gayle Cook Professor of Neurobiology at Weinberg. Her team applied the artificial signals to slices of mouse cerebellum.

The results showed that the electrical spikes matched key biological properties, including their timing and duration. These signals reliably activated real neurons and triggered neural circuits in a way similar to natural brain activity.

"Other labs have tried to make artificial neurons with organic materials, and they spiked too slowly," Hersam said. "Or they used metal oxides, which are too fast. We are within a temporal range that was not previously demonstrated for artificial neurons. You can see the living neurons respond to our artificial neuron. So, we've demonstrated signals that are not only the right timescale but also the right spike shape to interact directly with living neurons."

Low-Cost, Sustainable Manufacturing and AI Implications

Beyond performance, the new approach offers environmental and practical advantages. The manufacturing process is simple and inexpensive, and the additive printing method places material only where it is needed, reducing waste.

Improving energy efficiency is especially important as artificial intelligence systems grow more demanding. Large data centers already consume vast amounts of power and require significant water for cooling.

"To meet the energy demands of AI, tech companies are building gigawatt data centers powered by dedicated nuclear power plants," Hersam said. "It is evident that this massive power consumption will limit further scaling of computing since it's hard to imagine a next-generation data center requiring 100 nuclear power plants. The other issue is that when you're dissipating gigawatts of power, there's a lot of heat. Because data centers are cooled with water, AI is putting severe stress on the water supply. However you look at it, we need to come up with more energy-efficient hardware for AI."

The study, "Multi-order complexity spiking neurons enabled by printed MoS2 memristive nanosheet networks," was supported by the National Science Foundation.

Materials provided by Northwestern University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10738. Viral monkey Punch is now caring for a baby monkey after being rejected by his own mom

Punch, the viral monkey from Japan, is now looking after another monkey as Ichikawa Zoo has given an update on his well-being.

Back at the start of 2026, the internet found a new animal to rally behind – Punch. The now-infamous Japanese macaque went viral after news that his mother had abandoned him within the troop at the Ichikawa City Zoological and Botanical Garden in Chiba Prefecture, Japan.

Without a mother to cling to, Punch found solace in a stuffed orangutan toy that had been given to him by the zookeepers. Images of him hugging the toy quickly went viral online, with fans from all over the world wanting to visit Punch.

Over the last few months, things with Punch have settled down, but the Ichikawa City Zoo has given updates on his life from time to time – including when he found himself a girlfriend.

On November 8, the zoo uploaded a new video of the viral macaque, noting that he now cares for another monkey within the group.

In the video, Punch can be seen hugging onto the infant monkey, as well as grooming their head and neck, too.

“Punch and the other animals’ conditions change from day to day. We observe them, interact with them, and provide care, and we also adjust their feeding to suit the season and weather,” they said. “Punch is able to live so healthily thanks to the care our keepers provide.”

A number of commenters called it an “adorable” update on Punch, with many of them glad that he has been able to fit a spot within the group after being an outsider.

#10735. Witcher 3 Geralt actor approves AI use for his return after stroke changed his voice

Geralt’s Polish voice actor has approved AI assistance for his return in The Witcher 3’s new expansion after a stroke changed the sound of his voice.

Jacek Rozenek has voiced the monster hunter in Polish since CD Projekt Red’s first Witcher game, recording around 10,000 lines for The Witcher 3 before returning for its Hearts of Stone and Blood and Wine expansions.

However, a stroke in 2019 left Rozenek unable to speak for months, and although he eventually returned to acting, changes to his voice presented a challenge for the upcoming Songs of the Past expansion.

This ultimately led CD Projekt Red and Rozenek to seek help from AI, with the aim of making his new dialogue sound consistent with his original performance as Geralt.

Speaking to Game File, CD Projekt Red localization producer Mikołaj Szwed explained that the team needed to hear how Rozenek’s voice had changed following both his stroke and the years since his original recordings.

According to the report, Rozenek’s voice sounded much lower and gruffer following his stroke, which created a potential mismatch between Geralt’s existing dialogue and his new lines.

That difference would be particularly noticeable because Songs of the Past takes place around the same period as The Witcher 3’s main story. Players would also hear new battle cries and reactions while exploring existing areas, making the difference between Rozenek’s original recordings and his new performance more noticeable.

With Rozenek’s blessing, CD Projekt Red decided to contact an AI company it had worked with previously to help address the difference.

His return follows years of rehabilitation, during which he trained his voice for six or seven hours a day. Rozenek previously recorded dialogue in 2021 for a quest included in The Witcher 3’s 2022 next-gen update, although even that smaller recording session proved demanding.

The Witcher 3 devs fixed Roach in the remaster but will let players undo it

Witcher 3 Songs of the Past expansion officially announced

Witcher’s Geralt actor wants to sue AI platforms using his voice “into the ground”

“I was so exhausted after this recording,” he told Game File. “The point is that this voice should be as close as possible to my voice from 12 years ago,” he says. “I have great trust…for the creators of this game.”

The Witcher 3: Wild Hunt – Songs of the Past expansion is set to be released in 2027.

#10734. AI swarms could hijack democracy without anyone noticing

A new kind of political threat may be emerging, and it is far less visible than protests or traditional voter manipulation. Researchers warn that highly realistic AI-controlled personas could soon play a major role in shaping public opinion and influencing democratic systems.

A recent policy forum paper published in Science describes how large groups of AI-generated personas can convincingly imitate human behavior online. These systems can enter digital communities, participate in discussions, and influence viewpoints at extraordinary speed. Unlike earlier bot networks, these AI agents can coordinate instantly, respond to feedback, and maintain consistent narratives across thousands of accounts.

Rapid progress in large language models and multi-agent systems has made it possible for a single operator to manage vast networks of AI "voices." These personas can appear authentic, adopt local language and tone, and interact in ways that feel natural to other users.

They are also capable of running millions of small-scale experiments to determine which messages are most persuasive. This allows them to refine their communication strategies in real time and generate what appears to be widespread public agreement. In reality, that consensus is artificially created and designed to influence political discussions.

Deepfakes and fake news signal early risks

Although fully developed AI swarms are still largely theoretical, researchers say there are already warning signs. These include AI-generated deepfakes and fake news outlets that have influenced recent election conversations in countries such as the United States, Taiwan, Indonesia, and India, according to UBC computer scientist Dr. Kevin Leyton-Brown.

At the same time, monitoring organizations have identified pro-Kremlin networks spreading large volumes of online content. This activity is believed to be aimed at shaping the data used to train future AI systems, potentially influencing how those systems behave and what information they prioritize.

Experts warn of growing impact on democracy

Looking ahead, experts believe AI swarms could significantly affect the balance of power in democratic societies. Dr. Leyton-Brown cautioned that these systems are likely to change how people trust information online. "We shouldn't imagine that society will remain unchanged as these systems emerge. A likely result is decreased trust of unknown voices on social media, which could empower celebrities and make it harder for grassroots messages to break through."

Researchers suggest that upcoming elections may serve as a critical test for this technology. The key challenge will be recognizing and responding to these AI-driven influence campaigns before they become too widespread to control.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A few years ago, animating a ten-second scene meant storyboards, rigging and a lot of patience. Today you can type a sentence, upload a drawing or point at a reference clip and get moving footage back in a minute or two. The results are uneven, but they are good enough that designers, marketers and indie developers now use them for storyboards, product teasers, game trailers and social content.

The catch is that these tools are not interchangeable. Some excel at cinematic camera moves, some at editing footage you already have, and some at a particular visual style. This guide compares five AI animation generators, what each one is best at and where it falls short. Plans, credits and model names change quickly, so check each vendor's pricing page before you commit.

Runway is a cloud workspace for generating and editing video, images and audio in one place. Its current video model is Gen-4.5, which Runway promotes for motion quality, prompt adherence and visual fidelity. The platform also offers agent and workflow tools, mobile apps for iPhone and Android, and an API for developers.

Best for: Creators who want to generate a shot and then keep refining it in the same environment, and teams that need an API.

Limitations: There is a lot to learn, and credit costs add up when you iterate. Runway offers a free trial, but check the pricing page for current limits.

Kling AI is an AI video and image generator with a creative studio for image generation, video generation and motion control. It also includes an agent for brainstorming and scripting, and a node-based canvas for working through scripts, storyboards, images and video with multiple rounds of editing. Kling lists advertising, film, music videos and e-commerce among its use cases, and offers an API platform and automation options for producing video at scale.

Best for: Marketers and studios that want to move from script to storyboard to footage in a single workspace.

Limitations: The feature set is broad, so newcomers may need time to find the right workflow. Free credits were not described on the pages I checked, so confirm what is available before relying on it.

Luma focuses on turning still images into video with camera motion. Its workspace can create transitions between frames, extend clips, transform footage into new styles, reframe aspect ratios and add voice, music, captions and lip sync. It runs Luma's own Ray models and also gives access to several third-party models, so you can compare results without switching platforms.

Best for: Designers and illustrators who already have key art, product shots or concept images and want to bring them to life.

Limitations: Paid plans start at around $30 a month at the time of writing, and generation credits can run out quickly if you test many variations.

Pika is a creative platform covering video, images and audio. Its Video Studio can produce anything from a short clip to a multi-shot video of around 30 seconds, and its toolkit includes Extend Video, Motion Transfer, Swap Anything and Color Grade. Character Studio lets you create reusable characters, and separate studios handle music, speech and sound effects. Pika also exposes several video models, including its own Pika 2.5 alongside third-party options.

Best for: Social content creators who want effects, sound and edits in one tool.

Limitations: With so many tools and models, results vary from one to the next. Check the current pricing page for credit details.

Pollo AI is a generator that leans into one look: anime. Its animation generator creates anime-style videos from a text prompt and lets you adjust the video content, output style and size. The page offers eleven Japanese animator-inspired style options, such as Hayao Miyazaki and Makoto Shinkai, and the platform also provides a separate tool that converts existing video into anime.

Best for: Creators who want a consistent anime look for shorts, fan projects, music visuals or social posts without learning a full editing suite.

Limitations: The generator page describes text prompts as its input and does not state clip length, resolution or credit details, so test a few generations and review the terms before planning a project around it. As with any style-focused tool, the look is distinctive, which is a strength for anime projects and a limit for anything else.

Start with the job rather than the brand. If you need to edit and refine footage, Runway is a natural fit. If you want a script-to-video pipeline, look at Kling. If you have strong still images, try Luma. If you want effects and sound for social posts, Pika covers a lot of ground. If anime is the goal, Pollo AI is built for it.

Run the same prompt through two or three tools before paying for anything. Differences in motion, faces and text rendering show up quickly, and they are often more important than the feature list.

AI animation tools have moved from novelty to practical production aid. None of them replaces a skilled animator, but each can speed up storyboards, concepts and short-form content when matched to the right job. Try a few with a real project, compare the output, and keep a human in charge of the final cut. If you want a quick way to experiment with anime styles, you can create anime clips from a prompt and see how the results compare with the other tools on this list.

Are AI animation generators free? Most offer a free trial or limited credits, with paid plans for more generations and higher quality. Terms change often, so read each pricing page.

Can I use AI-generated animation commercially? It depends on the provider's terms and your plan. Check the licensing section before publishing client work.

Do I need animation experience? No, but knowing basic shot composition helps you write better prompts and judge the results.

Which tool is best for beginners? Pick one with a simple prompt-first workflow, test the free tier and move to a more advanced tool as your needs grow.

#10728. This AI knew the answers but didn’t understand the questions

Psychologists have long debated whether the human mind can be explained by a single, unified theory or if different functions such as attention and memory must be studied separately. Now, artificial intelligence (AI) is entering that debate, offering a new way to explore how the mind works.

In July 2025, a study published in Nature introduced an AI model called "Centaur." Built on standard large language models and refined using data from psychological experiments, Centaur was designed to simulate human cognitive behavior. It reportedly performed well across 160 tasks, including decision-making, executive control, and other mental processes. The results drew widespread attention and were seen as a possible step toward AI systems that could replicate human thinking more broadly.

A more recent study published in National Science Open challenges those claims. Researchers from Zhejiang University argue that Centaur's apparent success may come from overfitting. In other words, instead of understanding the tasks, the model may have learned to recognize patterns in the training data and reproduce expected answers.

To test this idea, the researchers created several new evaluation scenarios. In one example, they replaced the original multiple-choice prompts, which described specific psychological tasks, with the instruction "Please choose option A." If the model truly understood the task, it should have consistently selected option A. Instead, Centaur continued to choose the "correct answers" from the original dataset.

This behavior suggests that the model was not interpreting the meaning of the questions. Rather, it relied on learned statistical patterns to "guess" answers. The researchers compared this to a student who scores well by memorizing test formats without actually understanding the material.

The findings highlight the need for caution when assessing the abilities of large language models. While these systems can be highly effective at fitting data, their "black-box" nature makes it difficult to know how they arrive at their outputs. This can lead to issues such as hallucinations or misinterpretations. Careful and varied testing is essential to determine whether a model truly has the skills it appears to demonstrate.

The Real Challenge: Language Understanding

Although Centaur was presented as a model capable of simulating cognition, its biggest limitation appears to be in language comprehension. Specifically, it struggles to recognize and respond to the intent behind questions. The study suggests that achieving true language understanding may be one of the most important challenges in developing AI systems that can model human cognition more fully.

#10723. AI lets chemists design molecules by simply describing them

Creating new molecules is one of the toughest tasks in chemistry. Whether the goal is a life-saving drug or a cutting-edge material, each compound must be built through a carefully planned series of reactions. Mapping out these steps requires deep expertise and strategic thinking, which is why chemists often spend years mastering the process.

A major hurdle is retrosynthesis. In this approach, chemists begin with the final molecule they want and work backward to figure out simpler starting materials and possible reaction routes. This involves many decisions, such as selecting the right building blocks, deciding when to form rings, and determining whether sensitive parts of the molecule need protection. While computers can scan enormous "chemical spaces," they still struggle to match the strategic judgment of experienced chemists.

Another challenge involves reaction mechanisms, which describe how reactions proceed step by step through the movement of electrons. Understanding these mechanisms allows scientists to predict new reactions, improve efficiency, and avoid costly trial and error. Although current computational tools can suggest many possible pathways, they often lack the intuition needed to pinpoint the most realistic ones.

Researchers led by Philippe Schwaller at EPFL have developed a new method that uses large language models (LLMs) as reasoning tools for chemistry. Rather than directly generating chemical structures, these models act as evaluators that guide existing computational systems.

The new framework, called Synthegy, combines traditional search algorithms with AI that can interpret chemical strategies written in natural language.

"When making tools for chemists, the user interface matters a lot, and previous tools relied on cumbersome filters and rules," says Andres M Bran, the first author of the Synthegy paper published in Matter. "With Synthegy, we're giving chemists the power to just talk, allowing them to iterate much faster and navigate more complex synthetic ideas."

How Synthegy Improves Retrosynthesis Planning

Synthegy starts with a target molecule and a simple instruction written in everyday language. For example, a chemist might request that a specific ring be formed early or that unnecessary protecting groups be avoided. Standard retrosynthesis software then generates many possible pathways.

Each of these pathways is converted into text and reviewed by a language model. Synthegy scores how well each option matches the chemist's instructions and explains its reasoning. This makes it easier to rank and filter the best routes. By guiding searches with natural language, chemists can quickly focus on strategies that align with their goals.

Understanding Reaction Mechanisms With AI

Synthegy applies a similar method to reaction mechanisms. It breaks reactions down into basic electron movements and explores different possibilities. The language model evaluates each step and steers the search toward pathways that make chemical sense.

The system can also incorporate additional details, such as reaction conditions or expert hypotheses, provided as text. This flexibility allows researchers to refine their analysis and explore more realistic scenarios.

In synthesis planning, Synthgey was able to identify pathways that matched complex strategic instructions. In a double-blind study, 36 chemists provided 368 valid evaluations, and their assessments agreed with the system's results 71.2% of the time on average.

The framework can flag unnecessary protecting steps, judge how feasible reactions are, and prioritize efficient solutions. It also demonstrates that LLMs can operate at multiple levels, from analyzing functional groups to evaluating entire synthetic routes. Larger models performed best, while smaller ones showed more limited abilities.

This research highlights a different way AI can support chemistry. Instead of replacing human decision-making, Synthegy positions language models as guides that help interpret and refine computational results. Chemists can describe their goals in plain language and receive solutions that reflect their strategy.

The approach could speed up drug discovery, improve reaction design, and make advanced tools more accessible to scientists.

"The connection between synthesis planning and mechanisms is very exciting: we usually use mechanisms to discover new reactions that enable us to synthesize new molecules," says Andres M Bran. "Our work is bridging that gap computationally through a unified natural language interface."

#10722. A 10-Point Checklist for Legacy Application Modernization

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Legacy applications often support critical business operations, but keeping them running can become increasingly difficult. Outdated infrastructure, growing technical debt, security vulnerabilities, and limited integration capabilities can make these systems expensive to maintain and harder to adapt to changing business needs.

Modernizing them involves more than upgrading technology. You need to understand what the existing application does, identify which components need improvement, and manage the transition without disrupting essential operations.

This 10-point legacy application modernization checklist walks you through the process, from assessing your existing systems and selecting a modernization strategy to migrating data, testing applications, and measuring results.

Legacy application modernization is the process of updating existing software applications, their architecture, infrastructure, or underlying technologies to meet current business and technical requirements.

Depending on the application's condition, modernization may involve moving it to a new platform, improving its code, redesigning its architecture, or replacing it with a new solution.

The goal is to improve performance, security, scalability, maintainability, and the application's ability to support future business requirements.

Modernization isn't necessarily the same as cloud migration. Moving an application from on-premises infrastructure to the cloud changes where it runs, but it may leave its underlying architecture and technical limitations unchanged.

An organization can modernize an application without moving it to the cloud or combine both approaches as part of a larger transformation.

Use this checklist to track the ten essential steps involved in modernizing legacy applications.

1. Define modernization goals: Identify business problems and establish measurable success criteria.

2. Assess existing applications: Review infrastructure, technical debt, code quality, and performance limitations.

3. Map application dependencies: Document critical workflows, integrations, and business rules.

4. Choose a modernization strategy: Select an approach based on business value, cost, complexity, and risk.

5. Develop a roadmap and budget: Prioritize activities, estimate costs, assign responsibilities, and establish milestones.

6. Plan architecture and security: Design the target environment and address security, compliance, and availability requirements.

7. Prepare data migration: Plan data transfer, integration changes, and validation procedures.

8. Modernize incrementally: Validate the approach with a pilot and implement changes in manageable phases.

9. Test and deploy: Verify functionality, performance, security, and data integrity before production rollout.

10. Monitor and optimize: Measure results, support users, and continuously improve the modernized application.

Let's examine what each step involves and how to determine whether you're ready to proceed.

Before selecting technologies or planning development work, identify what the modernization initiative needs to achieve.

Clear business objectives help prioritize investments, guide technical decisions, and establish whether the project delivers meaningful improvements.

Start by examining the problems affecting your application's performance, reliability, or business value.

For example, a legacy order management system might still process transactions reliably but require extensive manual work whenever the business adds a new sales channel.

In this situation, improving integration capabilities may be more valuable than replacing the entire application.

An application's age alone doesn't justify a major technology investment. Whether legacy modernization makes sense for your business depends on the problems the existing system creates, the cost of maintaining it, and whether smaller improvements could resolve those issues.

Once you've identified the problems, translate them into measurable objectives.

If slow performance is the primary concern, establish a target for response times or transaction processing speed. If maintenance expenses are rising, determine what reduction in operating costs would justify the investment.

Relevant key performance indicators (KPIs) may include:

These baseline measurements will help determine whether the changes deliver the expected results.

Completion check: You have a documented business case identifying the problems to address, expected outcomes, measurable KPIs, and stakeholders responsible for approving success.

A legacy application assessment provides a clear picture of your current technology environment.

Without this assessment, teams may underestimate project complexity, overlook critical components, or choose a modernization approach that doesn't address the application's actual limitations.

Begin by creating an inventory of your applications and their supporting technologies.

Document each application's purpose, business owner, primary users, operating environment, programming languages, frameworks, databases, and infrastructure requirements.

Also identify software versions, third-party dependencies, licensing restrictions, and components approaching or past their end-of-support dates.

If your organization operates several legacy applications, use a consistent assessment framework. This makes it easier to compare their business importance, technical condition, and modernization urgency.

Review the application's codebase to identify issues that make maintenance and development difficult.

Look for tightly coupled components, duplicated logic, outdated libraries, insufficient automated tests, and undocumented configurations.

Pay particular attention to areas where small modifications regularly cause unexpected problems elsewhere in the system.

For example, if payment processing, inventory management, and customer records share tightly connected code, modifying one function could require extensive testing across the entire application.

Your infrastructure assessment should also cover hardware limitations, database compatibility, backup capabilities, operating systems, and unsupported dependencies.

Analyze how the application performs during normal operations and periods of increased demand.

Review response times, database queries, resource utilization, transaction volumes, and recurring failures.

Existing monitoring data and incident records can reveal problems that aren't immediately visible during routine use.

Determine whether performance limitations originate from inefficient application code, infrastructure capacity, database design, or external dependencies.

This distinction matters because adding computing resources won't necessarily resolve problems caused by poor application logic.

Completion check: You have an application inventory and technical assessment report documenting major components, technical debt, operational risks, and performance limitations.

Legacy applications rarely operate independently. They exchange data with other systems, support interconnected business processes, and often contain business rules that aren't fully documented.

Understanding these dependencies is essential because changing one component can affect several other applications or workflows.

Document the processes that rely on the application, including the steps users follow, the data they need, and the expected outcomes.

Work with business users, application owners, operations teams, and developers familiar with the system.

Identify processes that cannot tolerate significant interruption, such as payment processing, order fulfillment, customer authentication, or financial reporting.

Don't overlook activities that happen less frequently. Month-end financial reports, annual calculations, and scheduled data exchanges may depend on functionality that isn't exercised during everyday operations.

Map how the application communicates with databases, APIs, third-party services, and other internal systems.

Include scheduled jobs, file transfers, message queues, authentication services, and reporting tools.

Some legacy applications contain years of accumulated business logic.

Rules for pricing, approvals, calculations, or customer eligibility may exist only in source code, database procedures, or undocumented workflows.

Identify these rules before modifying the underlying implementation.

Where possible, create automated tests that capture existing expected behavior. These tests can help verify that the modernized application preserves required functionality.

Completion check: Critical workflows, system dependencies, integrations, and business rules are documented and reviewed by the relevant business and technical owners.

The appropriate modernization strategy depends on the application's technical condition, business importance, budget, and future requirements.

Not every application needs to be rebuilt. Some systems require targeted improvements, while others may be better replaced or retired.

Common legacy application modernization strategies include:

Different applications within the same organization may require different approaches.

Evaluate each option against your technical constraints and business requirements.

Consider implementation effort, expected benefits, ongoing maintenance costs, required expertise, and potential disruption.

For example, replatforming may be suitable for an application that operates reliably but depends on aging infrastructure. Rearchitecting may be necessary when tightly coupled components prevent independent updates or scaling.

A complete rebuild requires particular caution. Recreating undocumented functionality can be expensive, and important business rules may be lost if the existing system hasn't been thoroughly assessed.

The right strategy should solve the identified problems without introducing unnecessary cost or complexity.

Completion check: Each application has a selected modernization strategy, documented rationale, estimated effort, and an approved decision on whether to modernize, retain, replace, or retire it.

Once you've selected a modernization approach, convert it into an actionable plan.

Your roadmap should establish what will change, in what order, who will be responsible, and how progress will be evaluated.

Prioritize modernization work according to business value, technical complexity, urgency, and dependencies.

Applications with unsupported components or significant security risks may require early attention. However, starting with the most complex application isn't always the best choice.

A smaller, lower-risk application can provide an opportunity to validate tools, workflows, and team capabilities before modernizing more critical systems.

Dependencies also affect the sequence. For example, a shared authentication service may need to be updated before applications that rely on it can be migrated.

Prepare a budget covering the full modernization process rather than development alone.

Compare upfront implementation expenses with expected operating costs over time.

A modernization approach with a lower initial cost may still require significant ongoing maintenance.

Allow contingency for unexpected issues, particularly when legacy documentation is incomplete or system dependencies remain uncertain.

Divide the project into phases with defined deliverables and approval requirements.

Assign responsible owners for architecture, development, security, data migration, testing, and business acceptance.

Establish how project risks will be reported and who can approve changes to scope, budget, or deployment schedules.

Keep the roadmap adaptable. Findings from pilot implementations or migration rehearsals may require changes to the original plan.

Completion check: Your modernization roadmap includes prioritized activities, estimated costs, responsible owners, dependencies, milestones, and approval checkpoints.

The target architecture determines how the modernized application will operate, scale, integrate with other systems, and remain maintainable.

Security and compliance requirements should be incorporated into the design from the beginning.

Choose an architecture that fits the application's actual requirements.

Possible approaches include a modular monolith, service-oriented architecture, microservices, or event-driven components.

Microservices aren't automatically the best option. Although they allow independent deployment and scaling of components, they also introduce complexity in communication, monitoring, deployment, and data management.

For smaller applications or tightly integrated business processes, a well-structured modular architecture may be easier to maintain.

Document architectural decisions covering application components, data storage, interfaces, infrastructure requirements, and operational responsibilities.

Identify security weaknesses that should be resolved during modernization.

Review authentication, authorization, encryption, secrets management, logging, and access to sensitive information.

Evaluate third-party dependencies and establish processes for vulnerability scanning, patching, and security testing.

Regulatory and contractual requirements should also influence the target design.

Security controls should be applied throughout development and deployment rather than added shortly before release.

Define how the application will remain available and recover from failures.

Establish recovery time objectives (RTOs) and recovery point objectives (RPOs) based on business requirements.

Plan backup procedures, restoration testing, appropriate redundancy, and monitoring for critical components.

Validate that the target environment can support the required availability and recovery objectives.

Completion check: The target architecture has documented design decisions, security controls, compliance requirements, availability objectives, and recovery procedures.

Data migration is one of the most sensitive stages of legacy application modernization.

Legacy databases may contain inconsistent records, outdated formats, duplicated information, or relationships that newer systems handle differently.

A migration strategy needs to explain how data will be transferred, validated, and made available without compromising business operations.

Begin by reviewing the quality and structure of existing data.

Identify missing values, duplicate records, inconsistent formats, obsolete fields, and relationships that could create migration problems.

Document how existing data structures correspond to the target system.

For example, a legacy customer database might store contact details in a single field, while the new application requires separate fields for email addresses and phone numbers.

The migration process needs defined transformation rules to handle these differences.

Determine which records must be migrated, archived, retained for compliance, or excluded.

Choose a migration approach based on data volume, application availability requirements, and acceptable downtime.

Depending on the system, this may involve a scheduled migration during a maintenance window, staged transfers, or synchronization between old and new environments.

Identify integrations that will need modification.

Where applications exchange information through APIs, files, or messaging systems, verify that the modernized implementation supports the required data formats and business behavior.

If the legacy and modernized applications will operate simultaneously, define how data updates will be synchronized between them.

Test the migration process using representative datasets before transferring production data.

Validate record counts, field values, relationships, and critical business calculations.

For sensitive or business-critical information, use appropriate reconciliation methods, including integrity checks where applicable.

Document how inconsistencies will be identified and resolved.

Also establish what happens if migration fails or the application needs to return to its previous environment.

Completion check: Data mapping rules are approved, migration rehearsals have been completed, and reconciliation procedures can verify data accuracy.

Modernizing an entire application in a single major release can increase project risk, particularly when the system supports critical business operations.

An incremental approach allows teams to validate decisions, identify problems earlier, and limit the impact of failures.

Select a manageable component or workload to validate your modernization approach.

The pilot should test an important assumption, such as whether the target platform supports the application, whether a legacy integration can be replaced, or whether an architectural change improves performance.

Define success criteria before implementation.

A useful pilot should provide evidence that the approach is technically feasible and that the team can manage its operational requirements.

Use the findings to improve estimates, identify missing skills, and refine the broader implementation plan.

Divide the application into manageable migration or development units where its architecture allows.

One approach is the Strangler Fig pattern, which gradually replaces legacy functionality while the remaining parts of the original application continue operating.

For example, an organization might modernize customer notifications first, followed by reporting and then core transaction processing.

This can reduce the risks associated with replacing an entire system at once.

However, incremental modernization also requires careful coordination. Temporary integrations, duplicated components, and parallel systems can introduce complexity.

Each phase should have defined boundaries, validation requirements, and completion criteria.

Establish repeatable processes for developing, testing, and deploying application changes.

Continuous integration and continuous delivery (CI/CD) pipelines can automate builds, execute tests, and promote approved changes through environments.

Use version control, consistent configuration management, and infrastructure as code where appropriate.

Include security and quality checks in the delivery process to reduce the risk of introducing defects during frequent releases.

Completion check: The pilot has validated the selected approach, implementation is divided into manageable phases, and repeatable build, test, and deployment processes are established.

Modernization isn't complete when the new application runs successfully in a development environment.

It must perform the required business functions, communicate correctly with connected systems, and meet operational expectations before production rollout.

Develop a testing plan based on the application's critical workflows and identified risks.

Automate repeatable tests where practical, especially for critical business processes.

Compare results against the performance baselines and acceptance criteria established earlier.

Business users should verify that the modernized application supports their actual workflows.

User acceptance testing (UAT) should include routine operations, exception handling, reporting requirements, and tasks that depend on specialized business knowledge.

Use representative migrated data during testing.

Confirm that critical records, calculations, and reports remain accurate after the transition.

Resolve issues affecting essential operations before approving production deployment.

Prepare a documented deployment plan outlining the sequence of activities, responsible teams, communication channels, and expected service interruptions.

Choose a deployment method appropriate for the application, such as a phased rollout, canary release, or scheduled cutover.

Establish measurable conditions that would stop deployment or trigger a rollback.

Rollback planning must account for data changes made after cutover. Restoring an earlier application version doesn't automatically restore database consistency, especially if new transactions have already been processed.

Rehearse deployment and recovery procedures in a suitable non-production environment.

Completion check: Required tests have passed, business owners have approved acceptance criteria, and deployment, cutover, and recovery procedures have been validated.

The initial deployment marks the beginning of ongoing operation rather than the end of modernization.

The application needs continued monitoring and maintenance to ensure that expected improvements are sustained.

Compare post-modernization results against the baseline established at the beginning of the project.

Track metrics linked to your business objectives, such as application availability, maintenance costs, response times, deployment frequency, security vulnerabilities, and user satisfaction.

Use monitoring tools, logs, traces, and alerts to identify problems that may not be visible through basic availability checks.

Review performance after deployment and at agreed intervals to determine whether the modernization initiative is meeting its objectives.

Technical improvements may introduce changes to established workflows.

Provide relevant training, updated documentation, and support resources for users and operational teams.

Ensure that administrators and developers understand the new architecture, deployment processes, monitoring tools, and incident response procedures.

Collect user feedback to identify usability issues or missing functionality.

Use production data and operational feedback to guide further improvements.

Optimization may involve updating dependencies, improving database queries, adjusting infrastructure capacity, strengthening security controls, or simplifying application components.

Once replacement functionality is stable and approved, begin decommissioning components that are no longer needed.

Before retirement, confirm that dependent applications have been migrated, necessary records are retained, and archival or compliance obligations have been met.

Removing unused infrastructure and software can reduce maintenance costs and unnecessary security exposure.

Completion check: Monitoring and support responsibilities are established, modernization KPIs are reviewed, and unused legacy components have an approved retirement plan.

Organizations without the internal resources to manage legacy modernization may need support from an external software development company.

Depending on the project, a development partner can assist with application assessments, code refactoring, architecture redesign, system integration, data migration, and implementation.

Companies offering custom software development and application modernization services include:

Idea Maker: Provides custom software development and legacy application modernization services, including application assessments, code refactoring, architecture redesign, and cloud migration.

ScienceSoft: Offers custom software development, legacy software modernization, application reengineering, cloud migration, and system integration services.

Itransition: Provides custom application development, legacy application modernization, system integration, software migration, and post-modernization support.

Simform: Offers digital product engineering, software modernization, cloud and platform engineering, and data integration services.

These are examples of providers rather than a ranked comparison.

When evaluating a development partner, consider their experience with your existing technology stack, ability to handle complex integrations, approach to data security, and experience delivering projects with similar requirements.

Ask prospective providers how they assess legacy systems, manage migration risks, preserve critical business logic, and validate application functionality before deployment.

The main steps are defining business goals, assessing existing applications, mapping dependencies, choosing a modernization strategy, developing a roadmap, designing the target architecture, planning data migration, implementing changes, testing and deploying, and continuously optimizing the application.

The exact sequence may vary depending on the application's complexity and business requirements.

Start by evaluating the application's business value, technical condition, dependencies, and future requirements.

Rehosting or replatforming may be sufficient when the application functions well and infrastructure is the primary concern.

Refactoring or rearchitecting may be necessary when code quality or architectural limitations affect maintainability and scalability.

Rebuilding or replacing becomes an option when the existing implementation no longer meets business needs or would be too costly to maintain.

Compare expected benefits, implementation costs, risks, and ongoing maintenance requirements before making a decision.

Common risks include undocumented dependencies, data loss or corruption, unexpected downtime, security vulnerabilities, integration failures, and loss of essential business functionality.

Projects may also experience delays or budget overruns when technical complexity is underestimated.

These risks can be reduced through early assessment, phased implementation, migration rehearsals, comprehensive testing, and validated recovery procedures.

The timeline depends on the application's size, architecture, technical debt, data complexity, integrations, and selected modernization approach.

A limited rehosting or refactoring project generally requires less effort than rebuilding a large application or modernizing several interconnected systems.

A reliable estimate requires an initial technical assessment. A pilot or proof of concept can help validate assumptions about implementation effort and potential challenges.

Measure success against the business and technical objectives established before implementation.

Relevant indicators include application performance, availability, maintenance costs, security improvements, deployment speed, and user satisfaction.

For example, if reducing maintenance effort was the primary goal, lower support costs and faster resolution of application issues may be more meaningful than infrastructure performance alone.

Modernization success should be evaluated over time rather than only immediately after deployment.

Legacy application modernization works best when it follows a structured plan based on business needs and the realities of existing technology.

The ten steps in this checklist cover the key decisions and activities involved, from assessing legacy systems and selecting a modernization strategy to migrating data, validating functionality, and maintaining the resulting application.

Careful dependency mapping, realistic planning, incremental implementation, and thorough testing help reduce risks throughout the transition.

Start by assessing your existing applications and defining what modernization needs to achieve. Those findings will help you identify which systems deserve attention, where to invest resources, and how to approach modernization without introducing unnecessary complexity.

According to the official job listing, the successful applicant will begin playing GTA 6 at midnight on November 19, streaming their entire playthrough on Twitch or YouTube.

#10718. Your “um” and pauses could reveal early dementia risk

The way people speak during ordinary conversations could offer valuable insight into brain health, according to new research from Baycrest, the University of Toronto, and York University. Scientists found that subtle speech characteristics, including pauses, filler words such as ('uh,' 'um'), and difficulty retrieving words, are closely connected to executive function, the group of mental abilities involved in memory, planning, attention, and flexible thinking.

The findings provide some of the strongest evidence so far linking natural speech patterns with key cognitive abilities. The work also expands on earlier research showing that older adults who speak more quickly tend to maintain stronger thinking skills over time (Wei et al., 2024).

"The message is clear: speech timing is more than just a matter of style, it's a sensitive indicator of brain health," says Dr. Jed Meltzer, Senior Scientist at Baycrest's Rotman Research Institute and senior author on this study, titled "Natural Speech Analysis Can Reveal Individual Differences in Executive Function Across the Adult Lifespan."

AI Analysis Reveals Hidden Cognitive Clues

For the study, participants were shown detailed images and asked to describe them in their own words. They also completed established tests designed to measure executive function.

Researchers then used artificial intelligence to examine the speech recordings in depth. The AI system detected hundreds of subtle speech features, including the length and frequency of pauses, the use of filler words, and timing-related patterns in speech. These markers consistently predicted how well participants performed on cognitive tests, even after researchers adjusted for factors such as age, sex, and education.

Executive function naturally weakens with age and is often affected during the early stages of dementia. However, standard cognitive testing can be difficult to repeat frequently because it takes time and people often improve simply from becoming familiar with the tests.

Natural speech may offer a simpler alternative. Because speaking is part of daily life, it can be measured repeatedly and unobtrusively on a large scale. Researchers also noted that speech provides valuable insight into processing speed and overall cognitive function in real-world situations, without requiring strict time limits that are common in many traditional cognitive assessments.

The team believes speech analysis could eventually become a practical way to identify people whose cognitive decline is progressing faster than expected and who may face a higher risk of developing dementia.

"This research sets the stage for exciting opportunities to develop tools that could help track cognitive changes in clinics or even at home. Early detection is critical for any cure or intervention, as dementia involves progressive degeneration of the brain that may be slowed," says Dr. Meltzer.

Future Research on Brain Health Monitoring

The researchers say more long-term studies are needed to follow changes in speech over time and distinguish normal aging from the earliest signs of disease. They also suggest that combining speech analysis with other health measures could make early detection of cognitive decline more accurate, practical, and widely available.

This research was supported by the Mitacs Accelerate program and the Natural Sciences and Engineering Research Council of Canada (NSERC).

Materials provided by Baycrest Corporate Centre for Geriatric Care. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10717. GTA 6 almost banned in Korea after government officials got to play it early

Grand Theft Auto 6 was almost denied a rating in South Korea, as officials asked to play the game early and see some parts of the map.

With November 19 getting closer and closer, Grand Theft Auto fans from around the world will soon finally get to play GTA 6. However, if you’re in South Korea, that opportunity was almost taken away from players.

As per a report from Inven Global, Rockstar Games’ newest title “came close” to not receiving a rating classification from officials. According to the report, an initial review of the game ended in a three-three between giving GTA 6 an ‘adults only’ rating and not rating it at all.

This review took place in early September, with requests being made to Rockstar so that officials could see parts of the game early and then vote again.

Initially, there were concerns about sexual scenes being out of context and “far too much genital exposure,” rather than the violence or crime side of GTA.

On top of that, security concerns about leaks meant that a playable build was not available to officials until the end of September, hence a delay in handing out a rating to the game.

As per Inven, officials also noted that the map is “so vast” that they had to make multiple requests to Rockstar to point out specific areas around any exposed characters. However, they found that the player “could not engage in any sexual interaction.”

Upon a later review, one member of the committee noted that: “It appears that they intentionally covered genital exposure in explicitly sexual scenes, while allowing exposure in background settings.”

Officials added that it would be a “massive controversy” if they refused to rate the game – which would effectively ban it from being released in Korea – as they’d be the only country to do so.
