#10565. Before Dark Souls there was King's Field, and five hours into brilliant homage Queen's Domain, it's already one of my favourite games this year

The clue, of course, is right there in the title. Developer Freshly Baked Games' Queen's Domain isn't just a retro-fantasy action RPG played in first-person; it's an explicit homage to King's Field, the long-dormant FromSoftware series that - as the precursor to Demon's Souls, Dark Souls, Bloodborne, Sekiro, Elden Ring, and all those other Soulslikes - helped shape the modern gaming landscape in its own small way. That, though, is pretty much where my knowledge of the series begins and ends - as much as I love FromSoftware, I've never actually played a King's Field game. I've no idea how Queen's Domain shapes up as Kings Field-like, then, but I do know that, five or so hours in, it's already one of my favourite games this year.

Queen's Domain begins, as many great games do, on a beach. Our hero, Prince Beriik, has been washed ashore after his expedition ship, on a voyage to the mysterious Isle of Neasied, is destroyed. Beriik is searching for his father, King Gius Abraon, who's vanished on a mission to save the kingdom of Bamaen - after years of peace, the evil Queen Siraatsu's army of the damned is stirring; the dead are rising, legendary beasts are returning, and the creatures of the land have turned feral. But for now, there's simply a beach, the cry of distant gulls, and the gentle breaking of surf upon the shore. Queen's Domain's lore might read like an explosion in a vowel factory, but you certainly can't fault its mood.

There's something, perhaps, of the superb Dread Delusion in Queen's Domain; specifically in the way both deploy the lo-fi video game aesthetics of the 90s and early 2000s to invoke a more deliberate kind mood: those unnatural low-poly angles, those shimmering lo-res textures, and those rasterised skies creating - when viewed en masse across an expansive interconnected world - a particularly woozy sort of landscape. Here, though, the vibe isn't so much putrescence as chilly isolation, the muted vistas awash in greens, greys, browns, and blues.

It's a mood sent further off-kilter by the wonderfully odd soundtrack, whose seeping retro synths shift unpredictably from ambient melancholy to thumping dissonance, drawing in everything from parping trumpets to quacking ducks. And then there's the geographic strangeness - the secretly labyrinthine shape of this deceptively compact island - that only grows more apparent the further you explore.

Once you pick yourself off the beach and wander inland, the path almost immediately forks: to the left a boarded trail, to the right a vast stone door. Up ahead a gated cave mouth, and looming high on the clifftop, a mysterious ancient tower. Queen's Domain is full of these kinds of curiosity piquing landmarks, serving as both orientation points and unspoken goals.

Queen's Domain isn't exactly what you'd call forthcoming, you see, and early on - in lieu of a more explicit objective - that tower seems like a promising lead. Rusty blade in hand, you plot a likely course, but while there's a sort of logic to the island from afar, the routes between its landmarks are woven in wonderfully convoluted ways. And before you know it, your path has spilled out onto the coast, winding a perilous cliffside route high above the crashing waves. A second option leads further inland, plunging downward into a shadowy glade. And it's only after battling an onslaught of ladybird-rat hybrids and unnerving chicken-crickets (Queen's Domain loves its weird wildlife amalgams) you realise the two routes converge.

From there, more branches, but always a tangled path; the island's deliberately disorientating design turning navigation and exploration into a fascinating puzzle. And that only increases once you find your first proper sword. Imbued with a powerful darting slash, it's both an offensive tool and - with a break-glass-in-case-of-emergency strafe - an occasionally defensive one. But, it also grants traversal powers, those darting thrusts carrying you across previously impassable gaps. Soon, exploration, initially restricted to ground level, grows increasingly vertiginous, opening up whole new possibilities. You'll bound across treetops, charting a circuitous path around the island, or return to those shattered bridges and see what lies beyond. And that 'what' has frequently managed to surprise me - at one point, an apparently simple ascent turns into a several-hour trawl through a water temple.

Paths aren't always obvious, either; you'll plunge into a pool of deadly miasma, shuttling forward at the last moment to propel yourself along a carefully concealed pipe, or investigate a previously unreachable overhang only to unearth a secret passageway leading to horrifying depths. And often, you'll stumble across sudden shortcuts, causing routes to unexpectedly fold back on themselves. Truthfully, the freedom is mostly an illusion - the island is carefully designed to prevent you from deviating too far - but the proliferation of secret corners, and the minimal handholding, means the paths you're forging feel like your own. And as you unlock new tools, you realise there's a Metroidvania aspect to these spaces too. It's clever stuff, particularly when more overt puzzles are introduced, feeding further into the traversal rhythms.

And throughout it all, combat serves as Queen's Domain's drumbeat, punctuating progress with encounters whose wilfully rudimentary systems do, admittedly, take a bit of getting used to. It's not just the deliberate wooliness of it all as Freshly Baked emulates the combat of video games yore, or enemies' merciless in-your-face lock-on once disturbed, it's the limited ways you can respond. You can swing your sword, step outside of an incoming attack (there's nothing so advanced as a dedicated dodge button), and, well, that's pretty much it. And even accounting for enemies' minimal, largely predictable attack patterns, you'll invariably fumble and die.

But slowly it evolves into something compelling; a methodical game of feints and opportunistic returns, of goading enemies into attacks so you can, as far as your stamina gauge allows, exploit the resulting window. It's still far baggier than, say, Dark Souls, but it finds an engaging rhythm. Partly that's down to a combat twist that actively encourages restraint: standard weapon swings barely do more than chip damage, but hold the button to charge up your attack, and you'll do substantially more harm. That turns combat into a dance of patient timing, as you try and find the perfect moment to unleash a swing as multiple enemies move in.

That's just the foundation though. Soon enough, you've got throwing knives (which slowly replenish over time) for kiting, supplementary damage, or picking off sentry-like foes at a distance. There are spears and bludgeons, often with unusual secondary abilities, and it all feeds into a rock-paper-scissors-style damage system that encourages experimentation with your arsenal - especially where bosses are concerned. And the final flourish is an automatic levelling system that doesn't just strengthen your character as you play, it levels individual weapons - eventually causing them to evolve. It encourages engagement with enemies rather than avoidance, and provides a regular sense of progress in a game where the sometimes unforgiving challenge means inevitable repetition.

Queen's Domain is not, I should stress, a Soulslike, despite some similarities. You don't drop stuff on death, meaning no desperate corpse runs, and while resting and replenishing your energy at crystals resets the world - encouraging some push-your-luck risk-taking - you can save your game independently, creating some welcome breathing room.

Put all this together and Queen's Domain is a wonderful surprise; a moody, sometimes silly retro throwback that, despite its self-imposed mechanical and aesthetic limitations, feels anything but limited. Five hours or so into its supposedly 40 hours of playtime, its mix of combat, exploration, and puzzling has settled into a properly compelling rhythm - and despite some tricky bits and my own cack-handed frustrations, it keeps pulling me back in again. I've no idea if Queen's Domain is a good King's Field homage, but so far at least, it's a bloody good time.

Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS5, Xbox Series X/S

#10564. Scientists recreate a bizarre quantum effect that stops a particle cold

A particle does not have to pass through a magnetic field to be influenced by it. That surprising feature of quantum physics has now been recreated by researchers at the University of Oxford, who used a hybrid quantum computer to demonstrate how an invisible magnetic influence can bring a simulated particle's movement to a complete stop.

The experiment reproduced a phenomenon known as the Aharonov-Bohm effect, in which a charged particle traveling around a region containing magnetic flux experiences a measurable change in its quantum state, even though its path never takes it through the magnetic field itself.

The Oxford team achieved this using a quantum system that combines qubits, the basic units of quantum information, with quantum oscillators, which represent vibrating motion. Their findings, published in Nature Physics, demonstrate how this combination could help scientists investigate fundamental interactions between matter and fields that are increasingly difficult to simulate using conventional computers.

A Strange Quantum Effect Predicted in 1959

In everyday physics, a charged particle responds to the electric and magnetic fields it encounters. Quantum mechanics, however, reveals that particles can behave in ways that have no straightforward counterpart in our ordinary experience.

In 1959, physicists Yakir Aharonov and David Bohm predicted that a charged particle could be affected by magnetic flux even if it traveled entirely outside the region containing the magnetic field. Rather than experiencing a conventional magnetic force along its path, the particle would undergo a change in its quantum phase.

Quantum phase describes an aspect of a particle's wave behavior that influences how different possible paths interfere with one another. Although the phase itself is not directly visible, differences in phase can produce measurable effects.

The prediction became known as the Aharonov-Bohm effect and was eventually verified in experiments involving real electrons. It remains one of the clearest demonstrations that the quantum world operates according to rules that go beyond the familiar laws of classical physics.

Researchers are now exploring how this phenomenon behaves in more complex theoretical systems known as lattice gauge theories.

These theories describe how matter interacts with fields associated with fundamental forces. They represent space as a grid, with matter located at individual points and fields associated with the connections between them. Such models are particularly important in particle physics and high-energy physics, where scientists study the basic particles and interactions that make up the universe.

The challenge is that the mathematics becomes extraordinarily demanding as these systems grow more complicated. Even powerful conventional computers can struggle to calculate how all the interacting components evolve.

Quantum computers offer a different approach. Instead of attempting to calculate every aspect of a quantum system using classical computing methods, scientists can construct another quantum system that follows the same underlying rules. They can then observe its behavior directly, an approach known as quantum simulation.

How Scientists Built the Quantum Experiment

The Oxford project began in 2022, when lead author Dr. Sebastian Saner, Dr. Oana Bazavan, and their colleagues started developing a way to simulate lattice gauge theories using a hybrid quantum system. They collaborated with Dr. Alejandro Bermudez of the Instituto de Física Teórica in Madrid.

Their experimental design relied on two different quantum components that performed separate functions.

The first consisted of qubits encoded in the internal states of trapped ions, which are electrically charged atoms held in place using electromagnetic fields. These qubits represented the gauge fields in the theoretical model.

The second component used quantum oscillators, created by the vibrations of those same trapped ions. These oscillators represented matter.

By combining the two, the researchers constructed a simple loop that served as a basic building block of a lattice gauge theory. Two oscillators represented matter at two different locations, while two qubits represented the fields connecting those locations.

Although the arrangement was small, it was sufficient to reproduce an important interaction between matter and magnetic flux.

Quantum Entanglement Creates a Surprising Solution

The researchers next placed the two qubits into an entangled quantum state. Entanglement is a phenomenon in which quantum systems become linked in ways that cannot be fully explained by classical physics.

Within the team's simulated lattice gauge theory, this entangled state represented magnetic flux passing through the center of the loop.

Initially, the researchers had planned to introduce the magnetic flux as a fixed background condition. However, creating the necessary interaction would have required capabilities that their quantum hardware did not possess.

Encoding the flux directly into the qubits provided a practical alternative. More importantly, it allowed the magnetic flux to become part of the evolving quantum system rather than remaining an unchanging external influence.

"For us, the exciting step was to encode the magnetic flux in a gauge field that was itself dynamical," says Dr. Saner. "Rather than having matter evolve in a fixed background, the matter and gauge field become part of the same quantum dynamics."

What began as a technical workaround ultimately enabled the researchers to explore a more interesting version of the phenomenon, one in which matter and the field could participate together in the quantum dynamics.

A Quantum Effect That Stops Particle Motion

With their simulated loop in place, the scientists examined how a matter particle moved between locations through a process known as quantum tunneling.

Quantum tunneling allows particles to move between states in ways that would not be possible under ordinary classical rules.

When the researchers ran their simulation without magnetic flux, the particle could tunnel freely around the loop.

Introducing the simulated flux produced a strikingly different result.

The particle had two possible paths around the loop, but the magnetic flux changed their relative quantum phases. As a result, the two paths interfered destructively, meaning their quantum contributions canceled each other out.

This interference completely suppressed the tunneling, leaving the simulated particle frozen in its original state.

The outcome demonstrated the Aharonov-Bohm effect within a lattice gauge theory containing a dynamical field. Rather than simply reproducing the familiar behavior of a particle moving around a fixed magnetic influence, the experiment showed how the phenomenon can emerge when matter and the field are both represented by interacting quantum components.

A New Tool for Exploring Fundamental Physics

The Oxford findings were published alongside a related study by researchers at the University of Maryland, led by Professor Norbert Linke.

The Maryland team also used a hybrid quantum system, but their experiment focused on simulating the Yukawa potential, a mathematical description of an interaction relevant to nuclear and particle physics.

Although the two research groups developed their methods independently, they later coordinated the submission of their studies to Nature Physics.

Together, the experiments demonstrate how hybrid quantum computers could become valuable tools for investigating fundamental interactions that are difficult to reproduce using classical simulations.

By combining different types of quantum components, researchers can build systems in which matter and fields are represented in ways that more closely reflect the physics they want to study.

The Oxford experiment provides an example of that potential. A phenomenon first predicted in 1959 and later observed with electrons has now been recreated in a quantum simulator where the field itself participates in the dynamics, offering scientists another way to explore the unusual rules governing the quantum world.

Materials provided by University of Oxford. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10562. Top 6 AI Image Generators in 2026: Features, Strengths, and Limits

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A designer who once spent an afternoon searching stock libraries can now describe the picture they want and have four versions in under a minute. AI image generators have moved from curiosity to everyday tool for blog headers, product mockups, ad concepts, game art and slide decks. The quality gap between tools has narrowed, but the differences that remain matter a lot: how well each follows a detailed prompt, whether it can render readable text, how it handles edits, and what you are allowed to do with the result.

This guide compares six of the best-known options, what each does well and where each falls short. Models, plans and limits change often, so check each vendor's own pages before you commit to a workflow.

OpenArt’s AI image generator puts several image models behind one interface. Its generator page lists models including GPT Image 2.5, Grok Imagine, Qwen Image 3, Nano Banana 2 and Seedream 5.0 Pro, and lets you create images from text prompts or reference photos. It also lists tools for camera angle control, upscaling, collages and a Character Builder for keeping characters consistent across scenes, with 4K export.

Best for: Creators who want to compare several models on the same prompt without juggling separate subscriptions and accounts.

Limitations: The page offers a free start but does not state credit amounts or plan details, and it does not address commercial use, so check the pricing and licensing pages before using results for client work. Output quality depends on which model you pick.

OpenAI's image generation in ChatGPT works inside the chat interface, which makes iteration natural: generate an image, then ask for changes in plain language. OpenAI highlights accurate text rendering, the ability to follow prompts with many distinct objects, and consistent characters across multi-turn edits. You can also upload reference images and have the model transform them. It handles both photorealistic and stylized looks.

Best for: Marketers and writers who want to describe, refine and re-describe an image without learning a new tool.

Limitations: OpenAI notes that tall images such as posters can be cropped too tightly, and that dense small text, multilingual text and precise graphs can still go wrong. Images can take up to a minute to render. All generated images carry C2PA metadata identifying their source.

Gemini's image generation runs on Google's Nano Banana models. Google highlights character consistency, text rendering in many languages, combining several photos, precise editing and 2K resolution. A free tier covers image generation and editing, while the most advanced model requires a Google AI plan. You start from the "Create images" option in the Gemini app, then prompt or upload a picture to edit.

Best for: People who need to keep the same subject across several images, or who want to edit existing photos quickly.

Limitations: Usage limits apply, and the strongest model is behind a paid plan. Images include an invisible SynthID watermark and a visible AI label, which is good for transparency but may not suit every use.

Midjourney is an independent research lab known for images with a strong, painterly look. Its official site points users to a sign-up flow and to a Discord community and help center for support. Many creators choose it for concept art, mood boards and illustration where style matters more than literal accuracy.

Best for: Artists, art directors and game designers who want rich, stylized results and enjoy exploring variations.

Limitations: I could not confirm current pricing, trial terms or feature details from Midjourney's public pages, so check them yourself before subscribing. The workflow can feel less like a normal app than the others on this list, and it is not the easiest route for precise, text-heavy graphics.

Adobe Firefly is built for professionals who already work in Creative Cloud. Adobe states that its Firefly models are commercially safe, trained on licensed Adobe Stock and public domain content, and outputs carry Content Credentials that show AI involvement. It offers text-to-image, image-to-image, sketch-to-image and style transfer, plus editing tools such as background removal, object removal, upscaling and generative expand. It connects with Photoshop, Illustrator, Lightroom and Adobe Express, and also lets you use partner models from other companies.

Best for: Designers and brand teams who need clear licensing and want AI inside the apps they already use.

Limitations: Plans work on a credit system, so heavy use adds up. Adobe lists a free tier with limited daily generations and paid plans starting at about $9.99 a month, but confirm current terms on its site.

Ideogram built its reputation on a problem that trips up many generators: readable text. Its current models are presented as producing clean typography in different layouts, with attention to prompt fidelity and reliable editing. It also offers an API with access to a catalog of models, and has released open weights for one earlier model for local use and customization under its license.

Best for: Posters, social graphics, logo concepts and anything where words are part of the image.

Limitations: The pages I checked did not describe a free plan, so look at current plans before relying on it. Like every generator, it still needs a human to proofread the text it produces.

Start with the job. If you want to compare several models side by side, try OpenArt. If you want to talk your way to a finished image, try ChatGPT or Gemini. If you need images that will sit inside a branded design workflow, Firefly is the safer fit. If you are chasing a distinctive look, Midjourney deserves a trial. If text must be perfect, test Ideogram.

Run the same prompt in two or three tools before paying. Differences in composition, hands, faces and text show up quickly, and they matter more than any feature list.

Reviews only go so far, because your own use case is what counts. Pick one realistic job, for example a 16:9 header image for a blog post about web performance, and run the same prompt through two or three tools. Write the prompt once and paste it unchanged, so the comparison is fair:

Then score each result on five questions: Did it follow every instruction? Are there odd details in hands, edges or objects? Is any text spelled correctly? Does it still look good when cropped to a square? Could you license it for your project? Ten minutes of this teaches more than any feature list, and it shows how much editing each tool needs before you can publish.

Most readers of this guide will put these images on web pages, where a few habits matter more than the generator you choose.

There is no single best AI image generator. The right pick depends on whether you value conversation, style, licensing, control or typography. Test two or three on a real project, compare the output, and keep a human in charge of the final choice. Whatever you generate, review it for errors and label AI-made images clearly where your audience would expect it.

Are AI-generated images free to use commercially? It depends on the provider and your plan. Read the licensing terms for the tool you use, and consider vendors that publish how their models were trained if you work for clients.

Which AI image generator is best for text in images? Ideogram and ChatGPT both highlight text rendering, and Gemini lists it as a feature. Test each with your own wording and proofread the result.

Do AI images have watermarks? Some do. OpenAI adds C2PA metadata, Google adds an invisible SynthID watermark and a visible label, and Adobe attaches Content Credentials.

Can I use more than one image model without separate accounts? Yes. Platforms such as OpenArt put several models in one interface, which makes side-by-side testing easier. Check the licensing terms for each model you use.

Should I add alt text to AI-generated images? Yes, when the image carries meaning. Describe what it shows or why it is there. Purely decorative images can use an empty alt attribute, following the W3C guidance.

#10561. Google’s original Nest Doorbell is broken for many right now

An issue with Google’s original Nest Doorbell has left live view of the video feed broken for many owners, and there’s no word on a fix just yet.

Users are reporting widely in the past few days that Live View video feeds from Nest Doorbells are broken in the Google Home app. The issue is affecting specifically the first-gen Nest Doorbell, which is best known as “Nest Hello,” but Google has been referring to it as “Nest Doorbell (wired, 1st gen).”

The issue has popped up in several Reddit threads in recent weeks, but seems to have spread much more widely in the past few days based on nearly a dozen Google support threads we found.

Some users also report that some first-gen Nest cameras are also not working properly, but we couldn’t find nearly as many reports of this.

Notably, Google did roll out an update to older Nest hardware at the end of October.

I'm so glad to see this article because I've been complaining and complaining to the void about this for weeks now. Google is taking NO responsibility to fix what they obviously broke. Internet searches say that I'm supposed to just upgrade the power supply but that makes no sense.

Interestingly, affected users say that Nest Doorbell notifications on Nest Hub displays are still working properly. Some have deduced that Google’s switch to WebRTC may be the cause of the problem, but it’s hard to say for certain seeing as workarounds based on that theory don’t reliably work.

Outside of generic troubleshooting messages, Google has yet to say anything about a fix, or even confirm there’s an issue. But, based on the number of reports, it’s very obvious there’s a problem on Google’s end.

#10559. In Charting the Unknown, Crimson Desert doubles down on its maximalism in audacious, faintly sinister style

And so we are back in Pywel: the super-sized and often super-pretty realm where the deserts are blood-red, the grass is a lurid shade of green, and Kliff and his merry gang of Greymanes seem to be pissed all the time. Yet what's this? Peace seems to have broken out across the region, which means our grizzled action hero is in a reflective mood.

At the outset of Charting the Unknown, Crimson Desert's recently released DLC, I'm tasked with taking Kliff out for a quiet spot of moonlit fishing. The problem is, I seem to have forgotten how to actually fish - and if memory serves, perhaps I never learned because the system was so poorly explained in the base game. So I flail around for a full 15 minutes before eventually relenting and consulting Reddit on how to snag a bite.

You might reasonably assume that these types of basic explanatory wrinkles had been ironed out in the countless updates since Crimson Desert's release in March. Certainly, developer Pearl Abyss has gone to great effort elsewhere to streamline what was, at times, a frustratingly clunky experience (those menus at launch, oh god) while adding even more stuff, such as all-new cutscenes (to improve the story's legibility) and the ability to ride wolves and tigers (because why the hell not). But for all the tinkering and wholesale additions, Crimson Desert has retained its essential character: the menus remain a total faff and the narrative, which I started afresh some weeks ago, is still bafflingly incoherent (maybe even more than I initially appreciated).

It's into this churn of ongoing development that Pearl Abyss has produced Charting the Unknown, a DLC which adds even more elements to a game already buckling under the weight of mechanical overload. Now you can build ships and sail them around Pywel's azure-blue waters. You can dive below the waves and swim with the fishes. You can also fall in love, get married, and - should things not pan out - get divorced. The everything game has become, well, even more everything in quintessentially audacious style. On that note, just wait until you see some of its supermassive Evangelion-esque maritime bosses.

Though don't get too excited. Like the base game, the DLC is a mixed bag. First, shipfaring lacks any kind of drama. Islands are so spread out as to make the actual journey painfully boring: impressionistic oceanic voyaging à la Wind Waker this is not. The diving, though, is beautifully executed. Underwater locales are notably serene, free from many of the irritating distractions that plague ground traversal.

As for the new narrative threads involving all three playable characters - grizzled Kliff, Bayonetta-alike Damiane, and gentle giant orc Oongka - they're equally uneven. Kliff's is the most interesting, transplanting the player to a desert cloaked in a thick orange sandstorm that, without the aid of a working compass, must be mapped out using beacons resembling luminescent dongs. It's pleasingly disorientating, a rare example of Pearl Abyss pushing a kind of intentional design friction, rather than accidentally creating it by inadequately explaining how to interact with the game.

Alas, there are also bugs, one of which was game-halting: Damiane's steed wouldn't spawn when I needed to follow Kliff on horseback. It was a reminder that for all the visual splendour, Crimson Desert can often feel like it's being held together by the code equivalent of gaffer tape.

Everything controls about as well as you'd expect if you've played the base game, i.e. not very well at all. Kliff may be able to run on water and soar through the sky like a cross between Matrix star Neo and Jesus, but he remains a bastard to control for even the most basic tasks. Why can't I lock onto the object a foot away? Why is he doing a three-hit combo somersault through the air when all I tried to do is pull out his lantern? These issues are compounded by the new romance and socialising options that appear every time you target an NPC: Crimson Desert becomes even fiddlier as it introduces these additional layers.

As the hours pass by - as I run, gallop, and bound through the arresting landscape in a manner that is comparatively frictionless compared to the rest of the game - my mind begins to numb. For all the extraneous mechanical gubbins, missions remain brainlessly simple: cavort from one waypoint to another and do a few simple button presses, or crack a few skulls to chug the narrative along. But then perhaps a certain numbing is the point of Crimson Desert? At a time when the real world is markedly inhospitable (diminishing job prospects, the ongoing cost-of-living crisis, the looming threat of geopolitical meltdown), Pearl Abyss' epic swaddles the player like a swords-and-sorcery comfort blanket. It is a safe space to disappear into for hours. Leave your worldly woes at the start screen.

The impression this mash-up of a game appears to want to impart is generosity: here, Pearl Abyss seems to say, is a product capable of accommodating your every wish, will, and whim. It appears to have left no gameplay stone unturned in its ultimate quest to provide the player with total satisfaction - a kind of blissed-out contentment at the thought of never having to leave this almost divine realm.

But rather than generous, perhaps there is something nefarious about Crimson Desert's maximalist design philosophy. It strikes me again that this is the most uniquely modern title I've ever played, as if the Grand Theft Auto and Assassin's Creed franchises, countless MMORPGs (the genre in which Pearl Abyss cut its teeth), and many hyper-compulsive mobile titles were squished together in an attention-devouring final form. One so mightily captivating, so singularly consuming, that the player keeps playing until their lifeforce is depleted and they can play no more.

That seems to be the way these "forever" games curve: toward the player's surrender - of mind, body, and time. And at least until Grand Theft Auto 6 is released in a few weeks, Crimson Desert and its new DLC seem to be the purest expression yet of this type of thing. Wading through its sheer mass of hit-or-miss distractions (which could expand even more), the design ethos begins to grate. As if I'm stuck in an elaborate, endlessly bifurcating maze of hamster wheels, always busy, though never enjoying any discernible pay-off. And so, as its presentational lustre wears off further, and its eye-popping massiveness becomes less of a novelty, Crimson Desert begins to hit differently: much less high-fantasy heaven and more like hell.

#10558. Physicists discover “astonishing” property of light that defies expectations

Scientists have discovered a surprising ability of light that allows it to develop spinning patterns as it moves through empty space, without relying on special lenses, mirrors, or materials. The finding reveals that light possesses a hidden geometric structure that can influence its behavior in ways researchers previously thought were difficult to achieve.

The discovery comes from an international team led by researchers at the University of East Anglia (UEA) in the United Kingdom, working with scientists in South Africa. Their experiments demonstrate that carefully prepared light can be controlled through its own internal geometry, offering a new approach to manipulating how light spins and twists.

The findings challenge long-standing assumptions about how different properties of light interact. They could eventually lead to more sensitive medical tests, faster and more secure communications, and advanced quantum technologies that use light to transmit and protect information.

Why the Direction of Light's Spin Matters

One of the most intriguing aspects of the discovery involves chirality, a scientific term describing the difference between left-handed and right-handed structures.

Human hands provide a familiar example. Although they are mirror images of each other, a left hand cannot be perfectly superimposed on a right hand. Many molecules have a similar property, including compounds used in medicines. Two versions of the same molecule can have nearly identical chemical structures yet produce very different effects in the body.

Scientists can distinguish these molecular forms by studying how they interact with light that has a particular rotational behavior. Traditionally, producing and controlling such light has often involved specialized materials, carefully designed surfaces, or powerful lenses that focus beams extremely tightly.

The research demonstrates that appropriately prepared light can develop this behavior naturally as it travels, without requiring those additional optical components.

"Our work shows that light can naturally develop this handed behavior all on its own," said Dr. Kayn Forbes from UEA's School of Chemistry, Pharmacy and Pharmacology.

"You just have to prepare it in the right way.

"Most people think of light as traveling in straight lines. But scientists can also create structured light -- light whose brightness, shape, and direction are carefully arranged.

"One extreme example is light that twists as it travels, forming a corkscrew shape known as an optical vortex. Each twist can carry information, making this kind of light valuable for high-speed internet, secure communications, and advanced sensors.

"Light can also spin as it travels, depending on how it is polarized. This spin can be left-handed or right-handed -- another form of chirality."

Scientists Discover How Light Develops a Hidden Spin

Light can exhibit different kinds of rotational behavior. An optical vortex, for example, has a wave pattern that twists around its direction of travel, much like a corkscrew. Light also has a property known as polarization, which describes how its electric field oscillates. Certain polarization patterns produce a form of rotation called spin.

Physicists have long studied the relationship between these two properties. However, the interactions responsible for the newly observed effect were generally considered extremely weak and difficult to detect without specialized experimental conditions.

The researchers found that the situation changes when a beam of light is prepared in a particular balanced configuration.

Under those conditions, the beam can initially have no spin, yet distinct regions with spinning behavior emerge as it travels through free space. The effect does not require the light to pass through engineered materials or additional optical devices.

"It starts off with no spin at all," explained MSc student Light Mkhumbuza, who carried out key experiments. "But as the beam travels forward, spinning regions appear and separate out -- almost as if the spin was hiding and then revealed itself."

Rather than creating spin through an external optical component, the researchers demonstrated how the beam's existing internal structure can determine where spinning regions develop during propagation.

To explain this unexpected behavior, the researchers turned to topology, a mathematical field concerned with properties that remain unchanged when objects are continuously stretched, bent, or reshaped.

Dr. Isaac Nape, from the University of the Witwatersrand in Johannesburg, South Africa, offered a simple way to understand the concept.

"To explain it, imagine a mug and a doughnut," he said. "You can morph one into the other without tearing it, because they both have one hole. That hole is a topological feature."

The researchers found that structured light possesses its own topological characteristics, encoded in the arrangement of its polarization.

This underlying pattern acts like a mathematical fingerprint. Even as the light beam changes shape while traveling, its topological structure remains, influencing how different regions of the beam evolve.

As a result, the arrangement of the light itself can determine where its left-handed and right-handed spinning patterns become visible.

The discovery gives scientists a way to guide this behavior by designing the beam's initial structure, rather than depending on specialized materials to produce the desired effect.

"This gives us a completely new tuning knob for light. By adjusting its topology, we can decide how and where chirality appears," said Dr. Nape.

Potential Breakthroughs in Medicine and Communications

The ability to control light through its geometry could eventually make several important optical technologies simpler, more affordable, and more versatile.

One particularly promising application involves medical diagnostics and pharmaceutical research. Because many biologically important molecules have left-handed and right-handed forms, carefully structured light could help scientists identify these differences with greater sensitivity. Such tools could be valuable for evaluating drug safety and detecting biological markers associated with disease.

The same principles could support smaller optical sensors capable of identifying chemicals or biological substances without requiring large, sophisticated laboratory instruments.

Communications technology could also benefit. By encoding information into different twisting and spinning patterns, researchers may be able to increase the amount of data carried by individual light beams. This could contribute to faster optical communications, improved information security, and future quantum networks.

Other potential uses include manipulating microscopic particles, cells, and molecules with light, as well as developing more resilient quantum systems. Topological properties may help protect delicate quantum information against certain forms of noise and interference.

"The implications are wide-ranging," said Dr. Forbes. "This work could lead to simpler and more sensitive medical tests, especially in drug development.

"It could also be used to pack more information into laser beams -- boosting data capacity for communications, including future quantum networks.

"And because the effect doesn't rely on fragile materials or precision-engineered surfaces, it could be easier and cheaper to use in real-world technologies.

"This research could lay the foundations for a new generation of light-based technologies, by showing that light's behavior can be controlled using its own internal geometry," he added.

These applications remain possibilities for future development, but the underlying discovery provides researchers with another way to design and control the behavior of light.

A Hidden Property of Light Finally Revealed

For decades, scientists have relied on increasingly sophisticated optical equipment to produce and manipulate unusual forms of light. The findings suggest that some of these behaviors can instead arise from carefully designed structures already encoded within a light beam.

By showing how topology governs the emergence of spinning regions during propagation, the team has revealed a previously underappreciated connection between light's geometry and its physical behavior.

The discovery also illustrates how much remains to be learned about something as familiar and extensively studied as light.

"For something so familiar, light is proving to be far richer, stranger and more powerful than anyone imagined," said Dr. Forbes.

"And astonishingly, this new behavior has been there all along -- just waiting to be seen."

The research, 'Topological Control of Chirality and Spin with Structured Light', was published in the journal Light: Science & Applications.

Materials provided by University of East Anglia. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10555. What we've been playing - "Sniffing out the warehouses on Prawn Island"

A few of the things that have us hooked this week.

Well, hello! I am once again wearing the Bertie hat in your usual host's absence, so joyous welcomes to our regular feature in which we talk about what we've been playing in recent times. This week, Marie has been revisiting Zelda - although perhaps not the one you might expect - while Dom is rediscovering Vice City of the 80s. Kelsey is playing some very weird cards, Connor is back in WoW - possibly forever - and I'm equally smitten with a different RPG.

But enough about us, what about you? What have you been playing this week? Do tell all! And don't forget we have an entire archive of previous What We've Been Playings for your leisurely perusal, just in case you forgot what you were playing a year or a month or a week ago. Bye!

Once again, I am finally playing a game from my backlog that I should've played years ago, and that's the delightful albeit dark card game Inscryption. There's so much more to this than you'd first expect, and I'm surprised the community has been able to keep the game's best bits under wraps for so long. Ultimately, nothing could've prepared me for Act 2, and my only real qualm with the whole experience is that I wish Act 2 had its own endless roguelike mode; you'd never be able to pull me away from it.

The game falters a little at the start of Act 3, I'll admit, and I've found myself very frustrated by what is an otherwise relatively easy time. This is down to me missing some rather useful earlier cards that I've since learnt would've made this final stretch much breezier, but oh well. I will still take down the Uberbots one way or another for the love of the game!

I've sunk every free hour I have into World of Warcraft: Forever since its release on Wednesday night. As of writing I am Level 19, waiting for an escort quest to come up in Westfall. So far things seem largely the same as in the original MMO, though the little changes and improvements I've seen have firmly planted a smile on my face.

This includes the new weather system. Like many others I was caught in a snow storm in Dun Morogh this week, a seemingly random environmental transformation that drastically limits view distance and blankets your screen with the cold stuff. It adds a lot, I think, to the fantasy of the game. I'll be playing this all weekend and for the foreseeable future.

I'll let you guess why I'm playing this right now of all times. Ahead of Grand Theft Auto 6's launch, I've made it my unofficial job to internalise the map of Vice City. I'm spending time driving around the strip, familiarising myself with the docklands, going for a few rounds of golf up in Leaf Links, and sniffing out the warehouses on Prawn Island. I'm immensely curious how similar the map is in GTA 6, and can't wait to see what's changed in the interceding 40 years in-universe. It's even better having V-Rock blast Iron Maiden and Twisted Sister at me; over the past few evenings, I've been playing this extra loud so it drowns out the sound of local fireworks for the sake of my poor, terrified dog. I don't think he's a fan of Bruce 'the air raid siren' Dickinson's voice - but it's better than all the apocalyptic banging.

The cold winter nights are drawing in, which calls for blankets, a warm drink, and a nice peaceful game before bed. I don't have an exact reason for going back to Tears of the Kingdom. Perhaps it's me diving into Ocarina of Time that fuelled my curiosity about returning, but what's surprised me the most is there's still so much I didn't see or do in my first 190 hours with it.

I started again, cleared my save file, and it turns out I've completely forgotten most things about the game - which is by no means a complaint! I'm rather enjoying making the little discoveries that are either brand-new to me, or ones that make me go 'AH-HA, I remember you' (in the case of Gloom Hands, the reaction was 'NOPE, NOPE, NOPE, NOPE.')

I've also had another surprise. I play in handheld mode mostly, and ToTK was all looking a bit blurry on the Switch 2's screen. So I got the upgrade pack and suddenly everything became beautiful again - Link was in focus, the individual blades of grass in Hyrule Field were clearly whipping in the wind, and the crispy snow around Rito village almost sparkled. I don't often get upgrade packs for games I already own, but this one was worth it.

I suspect this won't be the last time you see me yapping about Queen's Domain here. I've already enthused about it elsewhere on the site this week, but I've managed to squeeze in a few more hours since then, and it continues to enthrall. Here's a small moment from my most recent go: I'd been fumbling about the Tower of Knowledge for a while at this point, dying slightly more than I'd like to those jabby gargoyle gits and lumbering chimera things, so I thought I'd have a little wander away from the violence and have an explore. That was when I noticed the gap on the bookshelf, wide enough to slot in the mysterious tome - the Ratty Bug Compendium - I'd been wondering about ever since I picked it up elsewhere some five hours earlier.

Moments later a secret passage had opened, leading out to a balcony overlooking the ocean. Treasure! Exciting! But just as I'm about to go back inside, I notice a little outcrop on the exterior of the tower, seemingly reachable with my sword's magic dashing power. So over I go, discovering an otherwise inaccessible route into an inner chamber. There, I find a lever, which I excitedly pull, only to be rewarded with... nothing at all. Until five minutes or so later, when I discover a secret panel has now opened up on the other side of the tower, granting me access to the treats within. In isolation, it's not a remarkable gaming anecdote, but Queen's Domain is just crammed with these little incremental secrets and surprises, full of clever design that makes exploration feel so rewarding. I've already spotted more enigmas waiting to be cracked, and if Queen's Domain keeps distracting me like this, I might never escape its pull.

#10554. Google Messages homepage redesign: Pinned chat avatars, bottom search, corner Gemini

In addition to the new app icon, Google Messages for Android is testing a big homepage redesign that has three core components.

Update 11/7: Instead of pinned chats just appearing at the top of the list, Google Messages is trying a redesign that significantly elevates them above all conversations.

This redesign takes advantage of the existing background layer that’s currently just home to the logo/wordmark, search, and account menu.

You’ll see large contact photos appear above all chats, which pushes the list of other conversations down the page. Like iMessage on iOS, this layout has the downside of not showing any history or last sent information.

Meanwhile, Google is testing another change where search becomes a centered floating action button (FAB) alongside new chat. When you’re scrolling down the page, you’ll see “Search” in a pill-shaped button, with the other button minimized.

Hate the idea of just the pinned "bobble head" look. I like being able to view message tidbits. Plus, it's directly copying Apple (enough said). I have to use an iPhone at my job. I hate the pinned bubble conversation look and more importantly it's function.

Otherwise, you’ll see a circular magnifying glass icon and “Start chat” in the pill. The animation that accompanies this change looks nice and feels alive, while boosting one-handed usability.

Google has replaced the Gemini FAB with a button in the top-right corner next to your profile image. This matches the design leveraged by Workspace apps on desktop and mobile.

These updates are currently in the beta channel and not yet widely rolled out to all testers. Some users have all aspects of the redesign, but it appears that Google is rolling out the various components in a piecemeal fashion.

#10553. AI can act exactly like a human, but we still don't think it's conscious

Even when artificial intelligence behaves exactly like a human, people are reluctant to consider it conscious, according to a new study from Ludwig Maximilian University of Munich (LMU).

As artificial intelligence becomes more advanced, reports of AI systems misleading users, working together, or appearing to pursue independent goals are becoming increasingly common. These behaviors often lead people to describe AI as though it can think, feel, or make decisions for itself. But does behaving like a human actually convince people that a machine has consciousness?

New research from LMU suggests that people make a clear distinction between displaying intelligent behavior and possessing a conscious mind, even when humans and AI act in precisely the same way.

How Scientists Tested Perceptions of AI Consciousness

Published in the journal Cognition, the study was led by Dr. Louis Longin of LMU's Chair of Philosophy of Mind, working alongside Dr. Bahador Bahrami, Professor Ophelia Deroy, and other researchers.

"Whereas previous studies have typically asked general questions about whether AI actually has mental states, our study is the first to directly compare how people attribute the same mental states to AI and humans behaving in exactly the same way, under identical circumstances," says Longin.

"We may be using mental terms to refer to AI, but this may only be because we lack better words," says Ophelia Deroy, philosopher and a senior author of the study. "After all, AI is trained to act and speak like a human, so these descriptions seem natural."

To investigate how people judge consciousness, the researchers conducted experiments involving nearly 1,100 participants. One group read brief descriptions of artificial agents that showed different levels of responsiveness to their environments. For example, some agents reacted to sounds or recognized the emotional states of others.

Another group encountered the same situations, except the characters were described as humans rather than AI systems. Participants then evaluated how conscious or aware each character seemed to be of its surroundings.

A Surprising Gap Between Human and AI Consciousness

The experiments revealed that responsiveness influenced how participants judged both humans and artificial intelligence. Characters that reacted more actively to their surroundings were generally considered more conscious and more aware.

However, a striking difference emerged when participants evaluated consciousness specifically. Even the most responsive AI was rated as less conscious than the least responsive human. In other words, identical behavior did not lead people to assign the same level of consciousness to humans and machines.

The results changed considerably when participants were asked about awareness instead. For this characteristic, judgments of humans and AI followed largely similar patterns, suggesting that people interpret awareness and consciousness in fundamentally different ways.

"There is a growing worry in public discussion that people will see AI behaving in a human-like way and start treating these systems as if they had human-like mental states," says Longin, lead author of the study. "We found something much more nuanced: People are quite willing to say that an AI notices things and is aware of its surroundings. But they clearly draw a line when it comes to the term 'consciousness'. People consistently attribute less consciousness to AI than humans -- even when their behavior is identical."

Although people sometimes describe AI as conscious when discussing its actions, the findings indicate that they still distinguish between artificial systems and human minds.

The researchers believe the difference between consciousness and awareness offers an important insight into how people understand artificial intelligence.

Awareness is a relatively neutral term that can describe an agent's ability to detect and respond to information in its environment. Consciousness, however, carries deeper implications about subjective experience and what it means to have an inner mental life.

Because awareness focuses more directly on observable behavior, people appear comfortable applying it to both humans and AI. Consciousness carries a different meaning, leading people to judge the two much less similarly.

The findings also highlight why the language used to discuss artificial intelligence matters, particularly as AI systems become increasingly capable of producing convincing human behavior.

"Our study teaches us lessons for how we communicate about AI," says Bahador Bahrami, who is also a senior author of the study and an expert in social neuroscience. "Companies and journalists often reach for hyped descriptions of AI models, and credit them with intentions, plans, or even moral conscience and hesitation. What our study shows is that everyday judgments are much more discriminating: People do not simply put AI and humans on the same mental scale. But the boundary also depends on which terms we use. For more neutral descriptors such as 'awareness', the distinction can largely disappear. That makes our choice of language important."

#10551. Despite promising a heady mix of Vampire Survivors and Warhammer fan-service, Warhammer Survivors is somehow less than the sum of its parts

You know my favourite thing about Vampire Survivors? The slow build of strength, the swelling power fantasy, the edging. Developer Poncle has proved itself incredibly adept at goading players into ever more ludicrous setups, demonstrating that with some patience, a canny eye for build-crafting, and some very rudimentary knowledge of your surroundings, even an old man armed with garlic can be an extinction level event for the amassing undead. Warhammer Survivors sadly never reaches those heights.

Created by Warhammer 40,000: Boltgun 2 studio Auroch Digital, Warhammer Survivors is - as its name suggests - an officially-licensed attempt to transplant developer Poncle's winning Vampire Survivors' formula to the Warhammer universe. Cue 40+ characters ripped from Warhammer lore getting the action-roguelike treatment, crossbows instead of clock lancets, rocket launchers instead of fire wands, bomb Squids instead of skeletons… you get the idea.

But this fusion doesn't really work. While Vampire Survivors was always a bit of an uphill battle from the start, it still hooked you with a thread that pulled you forward, tempting you with power-ups and other intriguing toys to play with en route. If hour one was a slog, hour five sparked curiosity, and hour 10 was a romp. In Warhammer Survivors, however, hour 10 feels like just as much of a slog as hour one. And even though Warhammer Survivors offers two alternate universes - the grimdark future of 40K and the old-school fantasy of Age of Sigmar, each a full game in its own right - this does little to alleviate the grind.

It's all so slow. Characters move slowly, upgrades come slowly, unlocking the mode that makes things move a bit faster comes slowly. Every inch feels like a mile, and the moreish power creep - tantalisingly propulsive in Vampire Survivors - is out of reach for so long per run here, it's discouraging. That's not to say there isn't any resolution to all the tension. Every now and then, you'll roll the right dice, appease the right gods, make the right moves. Your miniature pixel heroes will overcome the odds, and burn through the writhing mass of Orks, Skaven, Tyranids, Nurgle, or whatever other bastards are trying to get you down today.

The Survivors genre - sometimes known as 'bullet heaven' now - lives and dies on the strength of the over-powered nonsense that emerges as all your weapons and evolutions start to harmonise with each other in brutal synchronicity. Warhammer Survivors seems reluctant to let you get to this level, though. Even 30 hours in (!), some runs will end before they ever really get started - no matter how many skips, rerolls, or other chance-altering upgrades you've committed to your save file.

And because this is really two games glued into one, you have to do all the requisite grinding and levelling on both sides. With massive maps and impossibly slow character movement (especially on some of the bulkier characters), collecting all the items per location is more of a chore than it is fun. This wouldn't be so bad if these items were just curios, but in most cases they're pretty significant quality-of-life enhancers. Why are you hiding them from me, Auroch!?

It's almost impressive how Warhammer Survivors manages to feel like a step back from Poncle's original in many ways: yes, there's more content, but there appears to be a lack of understanding of why this genre immediately took off and spawned a myriad of imitators. The very worst weapons are the only ones available at the beginning, so unlocking new characters and armaments is necessary rather than a fun bonus. Lore is paper thin - in Vampire Survivors, we had a blank canvas and the lack of lore didn't really matter, but Warhammer is steeped in its own mythos, so relegating it to merely set dressing feels remiss. Some of the synergies between items and their evolutions don't really make sense. The maps are awkward and fussy to navigate. The locked requirements to take on stage-end bosses are even more cryptic than in Vampire Survivors. In almost all the ways that matter when it comes to fun, Warhammer Survivors pales in comparison to its forebear. This wouldn't be so egregious if the game wasn't trying to emulate it quite so completely, but it is - and as such, all the actually decent design choices hidden behind layers of frustration are marred.

As a Warhammer and Survivors fan, I've found myself floating back to the game, even if - sometimes - my eyes roll back into my head within minutes of my first run. But it's not a complete disaster: on those occasions where the stars do align, Warhammer Survivors finds its way to a power fantasy worthy of both franchises. You'll grab a Flamer early doors, then pick up the passive Glue to reduce incoming damage. You'll gain the Heavy Flamer, the Boltgun, and the projectile-multiplying Dice Cube, eventually unlocking the Heavy Bolter - slicing through enemies and all but melting the face off that pesky Prophet of Gork.

I just wish there wasn't so much friction in actually getting to that point. I imagine Warhammer Survivors is the sort of game I'll keep on my harddrive (and on multiple devices; it costs a fiver and it's on Game Pass) for a while. If you can endure the teething pains and engage with the wobblier systems, there's the usual Survivors payoff waiting for you as the screen fills with pixelated blood and countless helpless enemies fall to your blades. Maybe the stress of the early game even makes this catharsis even better. So even with its qualms, Warhammer Survivors is still an enjoyable, lo-fi stress reliever to snack on in between other, meatier games.

Android, Nintendo Switch, Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS5, Xbox One, Xbox Series X/S, iOS

#10550. Android tablets are back, but are they for you?

Android tablets have always been a bit of a touchy subject, but it’s abundantly clear that the form factor is making a comeback. But, as someone who’s a fan of the smaller sizes, are you actually using the gargantuan Android tablets that every company seems obsessed with? In other words – are you the customer for the Android tablets these brands are selling?

9to5Google Weekender by Ben Schoon is a part of our free newsletter that also includes ‘Inbox,’ a twice-weekly roundup of key stories from around the web by Daniel Bader. Sign up here to get Weekender delivered to your inbox every Sunday along with ‘Inbox’ on Tuesday and Thursday. You can find past issues here.

For a while there, Android tablets had hit what felt like a dead end. Bigger smartphones and cooling of the tablet market as a whole, along with the general sentiment that Apple was doing a way better job with the iPad anyway, left the category in a place that felt stagnant – and that’s the nice way of putting it.

But, over the past few years, Android tablets have had a renaissance.

New form factors, big releases from a variety of brands, and flagship-tier devices left and right. Despite Android’s push into foldables and still-huge displays on nearly every smartphone, tablets are very much back. Prior to RAMageddon’s worst impacts, tablet sales were growing for nearly everyone, and Android brands were seeing a larger slice of the pie.

Anyway, this is on my mind right now because I’ve had a lot of Android tablets arrive in my office in the past few weeks, capping off a lot of tablet announcements in the past few months. I just finished up with Redmagic Astra 2, a tablet I think fits the description of “Android’s iPad Mini OLED,” and I’m in the process of moving into tablets from Lenovo and Amazon. Our Jordan Floyd has also been testing out the Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra behind the scenes. And if there’s one trend amongst recent tablet releases, it’s that big tablets seem to be the primary focus among a lot of brands.

Take Amazon’s new Google Play-equipped Alexa Tablet lineup, for example.

The Alexa Tablet 12 is the crown jewel of the lineup, packing the most powerful specs and a premium anti-glare display. It’s the only one that gets a “Pro” moniker, while the 11-inch and 8-inch models are noticeable steps down. Unboxing the Alexa Tablet 8 this week, for example, I was immediately reminded this is not a premium device – the screen makes that clear without any hesitation whatsoever. It was a case of whiplash after setting up the Lenovo Yoga Tab Plus Gen 2 with its 4K display.

It’s a weird one for me. I’ve never been a big fan of these super-sized Android tablets (or iPads, for that matter). I can get behind a 10-inch or 11-inch model, but I find myself more at home on the smaller slates. They’re fantastic for travel as an alternative to draining my phone’s battery, while I find myself just reaching for a laptop instead if I want a bigger screen. A big tablet, regardless of specs, can rarely replace a proper laptop for me, so lugging both around just sounds like more trouble than it’s worth.

But, clearly, people are buying these bigger slates. Samsung appears to be seeing enough success with its mammoth Galaxy Tab S Ultra lineup to keep the 14.6-inch tablet around each year while the smaller sizes are now on an every-other-year cycle. Lenovo’s lineup is virtually all underpowered when the screen size goes down, aside from the Legion Tab that’s now $900.

Are you buying these bigger tablets? What are you using them for?

I imagine that most folks opting for a bigger tablet are using these in the place of a laptop. Thinner and lighter, longer battery life, etc. And in cases like the Alexa Tablet 12 Pro and its $550~ price tag, I get the appeal. Lenovo Yoga Tab Plus Gen 2 takes that down a notch for me at $799 – you’re getting into laptop territory there – but it still is a compelling-enough alternative if it fits your needs. The Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra at $1,400 plus a $200 keyboard, yeah I don’t get that one at all.

Let’s discuss! I want to know if you’re using an Android tablet, what you’re using it for, and what size you actually prefer.

Ladies and gents, we’ve finally got a new flagship in the US. Motorola Signature 27 is launching on November 19 and looks excellent, though its flashy spec sheet won’t come cheap at $1,599.

Finally, something good came out of Googlebook

Googlebook is a disaster, but there’s finally one good thing coming out of it in Henry Cavill’s new “Mustache Protocol” game which, supposedly, was built on Googlebook. I’ll bite my tongue there, but the accompanying video brought a needed bit of joy into my week.

I use an 8" Samsung. Not the best in specs, but fine for browsing and reading books. I had a Nexus 10 years ago and gave it to my sister-in-law as I rarely needed something that big. My wife also uses an 8" Samsung, mostly for reading.

#10548. World of Warcraft: Forever's magical opening week reminds us all about the power of the MMO, and the threat it poses to your free time

World of Warcraft: Forever is a time vampire. Since its release last Wednesday, I have spent 78 hours in Azeroth. Some of that I was AFK, sure. But for many of those hours, I was lost, happily, in the rolling hills and vast planes of Blizzard's MMO - now enhanced, beautified, and given a new social energy that makes the old feel wonderfully new.

The original World of Warcraft is so beloved, so influential, that I wasn't convinced Forever could build upon it without cheapening the overall experience; without creating glaring clashes between its new additions and legacy elements that, in some instances, are now over two decades old. And while there's still plenty I haven't seen after 78 hours and 35 levels, I can speak to one truth: WoW Forever is stunning work.

Perhaps it's best to start with WoW: Forever's most obvious changes: its new lighting and weather effects. People love to speak about getting lost in MMOs, being consumed by their digital worlds, but as much as I've enjoyed World of Warcraft in the past, its ageing graphics tech never managed to ensnare me that way. Forever's new visual elements, though, have gone a long way toward pulling me in.

As I look out to sea, waves roll across the ocean. Inland, water flows fast along the rivers of Stranglethorn Vale. These are largely cosmetic additions, sure, but they make Azeroth feel so much more real. Dun Morogh's snowstorms - likely the first exposure many players will have to Forever's new weather effects - transform a peaceful, picturesque landscape into something hostile at a moment's notice. It's the same for Tanris; its vast desert now blighted with sandstorms that bring raging life to an otherwise fairly barren zone.

World of Warcraft; Forever's enhancements go beyond visual, of course. Before launch, Blizzard described Forever as an opportunity to address imbalances, and to show a bit of love to underserved playstyles and classes. That's why I decided I'd play as a druid and work toward creating a balance caster: a ranged DPS druid far slower to level than a feral one, and far less useful as a magic DPS compared to a mage. In other words, an unoptimal choice for the truly silly.

I actually levelled a balance druid to max in Classic World of Warcraft some years back and it was an arduous process. In Forever, though, adjustments made to the spells and talents have made it much less frustrating so far. You do more damage, for starters, and alterations to the talent tree and unlockable skills mean you don't run into the sort of mana problems that crippled balance druids previously. From what I've seen, all the ugly duckling class specialisations - like Retribution Paladins and Enhancement Shamans - have received similar changes.

My interest had been similarly piqued before launch by the talk of WoW: Forever's new dungeons. I still need more time to understand how these additions affect the wider leveling process, but already I can see they've been crafted with care, fitting nicely in the gaps between the original dungeons on a progression level.

Hall of Thanes is a Ragefire Chasm equivalent for Alliance players, serving as a solid introduction to dungeons for newcomers before they head off to The Deadmines and beyond. Here, you delve into the bowels of Ironforge, navigating tombs and tunnels in search of treasure and tackling foes beneath the seat of dwarvish power. It's simple and clean like all early game Classic dungeons are, made with such faithfulness to the original vision that I could be convinced it was always present.

It's also a lovely narrative introduction to one of the world's many antagonists, the Dark Iron Dwarves. In the original World of Warcraft, players would first encounter them across a scattering of quests before being thrust towards Blackrock Mountain. Now, though, they show up early and immediately plant intrigue. What's the deal with these drilling machines? What about the fire elementals they're with?

Forever's new dungeons also introduce some well-crafted quests. Anyone who's played Classic WoW can recount the frustration of dungeons that, thanks to some awkward design, often require two separate completions to finish off. Blackfathom Deeps is a prime example; there are never enough twilight pendants to go around, forcing players to go back in for a second run if they want to tick it off. As such, many, myself included, don't bother. World of Warcraft: Forever, though - with some exceptions - appears to favour a one-and-done approach to dungeons. In the new Excavation Site: Wetlands, for instance, there are just enough raptors hidden in tall grass for everyone to complete their hides in one go. A little thing, but meaningful.

But beyond all this, perhaps the most wonderful thing about World of Warcraft: Forever is how full it feels. It's lively, bustling with action and excitement. Some of this is just that new MMO energy, but Blizzard's new mega server approach, which vastly increases the number of players you'll see doing quests compared to a regular server, is undoubtedly a significant factor too.

Every major city is packed with people having their own adventure, each quest zone is littered with heroes doing their bit to save the world. And this opens the door to social interactions, the beating heart of any MMO. The sight of dozens of players jumping off a zeppelin or flooding onto an elevator in unison is a joy, and grouping with strangers for an escort quest or an elite quest is a curative for digital hermits.

At one point, I find myself drawn into a conversation with a mage during a quest for troll ears. The mobs were hotly contested, and Horde players were quick to snatch tags. The few minutes I spent there, talking about what's going on and how we're finding the game were lovely, and World of Warcraft: Forever's set-up encourages so much more of this kind of thing.

So, while I probably have plenty more sleepless nights left to go before I max out my druid, World of Warcraft: Forever has made a strong early impression. It's taken the original game and expanded on it in genuinely meaningful ways, leaving the things that worked untouched while enriching the elements needed some love. It's a wonderful point to jump into the game if you're a new player, then, and a refreshed classic full of twists for the returning veteran. Whatever the coming months and years may bring, this opening week has been magical.

#10547. Android Auto cutting off older devices, now requires Android 11 or newer

Google has officially dropped support for some older Android versions in Android Auto, now requiring Android 11 or newer to function.

On a support page, Google says that Android 11.0 or newer is now required to use Android Auto:

An Android phone with Android 11.0 (Red Velvet Cake) or higher and an active data plan.

Previously, Google supported Android 9 and Android 10 for the in-car experience, a minimum requirement that itself was raised back in 2024 (but not enforced until 2025).

Some drivers started seeing a “Phone will no longer be supported” message in October 2026, but Google only updated its documentation this week. Android Auto 18.1, the latest app update, still supports Android 10.
